=== PAPER: How Does Regional Decoder Specialization Help Low-Resource ASR Based on the SLAM-ASR Framework? ===

000

How Does Regional Decoder Specialization Help Low-Resource ASR
Based on the SLAM-ASR Framework?

001
002

050
051
052

054

Anonymous ROCLING submission

011

Abstract

012

The SLAM-ASR recipe — a frozen speech encoder, a frozen large language model as the decoder, and a small trainable projector between
them — is a practical route for adding lowresource languages to speech systems, but the
choice of decoder within it has not been studied
under controlled conditions. We ask whether
pairing each language with a regionally specialized decoder improves recognition, using
the Tiny Aya family, whose four variants share
one base model and one tokenizer and differ
only in the regional weighting of their posttraining data. Across twelve languages, the
region-matched decoder is only 0.55 character
error rate (CER) better on average than mismatched regional decoders (Wilcoxon signedrank p = 0.97). This absence of a matcheddecoder advantage persists on held-out test sets,
where the difference is −0.09 CER against a
minimum detectable effect of 0.67. The advantage also does not grow with the amount
of target-language data the decoder saw: for
the median language, the matched variant saw
just 1.3 percentage points more target-language
data than the mismatched ones — at most 5.7 —
though up to 236 times more in relative terms,
and recognition accuracy tracks neither measure. What does matter is training the projector at all: on low-resource languages, it improves CER over off-the-shelf models by up
to 102.6, including a language supported by
neither frozen component. For practitioners, a
general-purpose multilingual decoder suffices,
and the effort is better spent on in-language
audio. We will release all checkpoints.

ASR (team et al., 2025) have expanded multilingual speech recognition to a growing number of languages. Despite this progress, substantial work remains on the breadth and diversity of low-resource
languages and dialects, which has motivated recent
studies of the failure modes of these systems in
low-resource settings (Imam et al., 2025b; Kyslyi
et al., 2026; Kumar et al., 2026; Bharathi et al.,
2026; Azis et al., 2026).
One recent direction addressing these constraints
is the SLAM-ASR architecture (Ma et al., 2024),
which connects a pretrained speech encoder to a
large language model (LLM) through a trainable
linear projector while keeping both pretrained models frozen. Follow-up work has probed the recipe’s
robustness (Kumar et al., 2025), its data requirements (Fong et al., 2025), encoder depth (Kolluri et al., 2026), language-family effects (Zhang
et al., 2026), and prompt sensitivity (Burdisso et al.,
2026). However, the effect of the decoder’s regional specialization has not been studied. The
Tiny Aya family (Salamanca et al., 2026) makes the
missing experiment possible: small multilingual
LLMs sharing a common base model and tokenizer,
where each regional variant differs only in the posttraining data reinforcing its region’s languages.

Keywords: speech recognition, SLAM-ASR,
low-resource languages, decoder specialization,
Tiny Aya

1

Introduction

Automatic speech recognition (ASR) models such
as Whisper (Radford et al., 2023) and Omnilingual

1

We ask three questions. RQ1: Does pairing a
language with its region-matched decoder improve
recognition over mismatched regional decoders or
a general-purpose one? RQ2: If there is an effect,
does it scale with how much of the target language
the decoder actually saw — the exposure dose,
rather than the region label? RQ3: Independent of
the decoder choice, what does training the projector
buy over off-the-shelf audio LLMs on low-resource
languages?

087

Our contributions, over twelve languages spanning four orders of magnitude of encoder pretraining data:

097

098
099

100

• The first controlled test of decoder regional
specialization in SLAM-ASR (RQ1). Matching a language to its regional decoder does not
measurably improve recognition. The result
holds on held-out test sets, where any effect is
smaller than 0.67 CER — the smallest difference the design can detect — and is supported
by a run-to-run noise floor measured from
replicate runs.

once. The Tiny Aya family isolates the remaining
variable: its variants share a base model and a tokenizer and differ only in the regional weighting of
their post-training mix.
Low-resource ASR. Surveys of African ASR describe a persistent shortage of transcribed audio
and of evaluation resources (Imam et al., 2025a,b),
recent work explores using ASR itself to bootstrap
corpora for languages such as Fongbe and Hausa
(Adjovi et al., 2026), and practitioner accounts report the same constraint (Klejch et al., 2025). Several of our study languages — including Marathi,
Urdu, Hausa, Amharic and Kreol Seselwa — do
not appear in the SLAM-ASR literature surveyed
above, and Kreol Seselwa appears unstudied in
this setting altogether; the checkpoints and transcripts we release provide baselines for them. The
projector-only recipe suits this setting precisely because it asks for so little: no encoder training, no
decoder training, and a component small enough
to train on one GPU.

• A measurement of the treatment itself (RQ2):
matched decoders saw up to 236 times the
target-language data of mismatched ones, and
recognition accuracy does not track this exposure.

• A practical positive result (RQ3): training the
projector improves over off-the-shelf audio
LLMs by up to 102.6 CER precisely on lowresource languages, including one supported
by neither frozen component.

• We will release all checkpoints and evaluation transcripts, so that the comparison can be
extended and the released models can serve
as baselines for further work on low-resource
ASR within the SLAM-ASR framework.

2

3

Method

Our implementation follows the Encoder–
Projector–Decoder architecture of SLAM-ASR
(Ma et al., 2024), illustrated in Figure 1. A frozen
encoder extracts acoustic representations from
the input speech; a trainable linear projector
downsamples and maps them into the decoder’s
embedding space; the frozen LLM decoder
autoregressively generates the transcription. Only
the projector is optimized, which learns the
alignment between speech representations and the
language-model embedding space with a minimal
number of trainable parameters.

125
126

150

Related Work

Projector-based ASR. Ma et al. (2024) showed
that a single trainable linear projector between a
frozen speech encoder and a frozen LLM reaches
competitive English recognition, and subsequent
work has probed its robustness (Kumar et al., 2025),
how much labelled audio it needs (Fong et al.,
2025), how far the encoder can be pruned (Kolluri et al., 2026), how language-family structure
affects it (Zhang et al., 2026), and its sensitivity to
the training prompt (Burdisso et al., 2026). What
these share is a fixed decoder.

Decoder specialization. We use the Tiny Aya
family (Salamanca et al., 2026) as the frozen decoder: four instruction-tuned variants of the same
3.35B-parameter multilingual base model, sharing one tokenizer, so that swapping one variant
for another changes only the composition of its
post-training data. Tiny Aya Global is post-trained
across all regions, while each regional variant
reweights the mix toward one cluster of regions:
Earth toward Europe, West Asia and Africa; Fire
toward South Asia; and Water toward Europe, West
Asia and Asia-Pacific. We assign each study language the region of its cluster in the report (Table 1).
English is the one case where the label and the actual exposure disagree — the Fire mix contains

Decoder selection. No prior work isolates what
the decoder’s training data contains. Ma et al.
(2024) ablate the decoder’s scale (TinyLlama-1.1B
against LLaMA-7B), base against instruction-tuned
checkpoints, and family (Phi-2, LLaMA, Vicuna)
on English, and Fong et al. (2025) compare EuroLLM and Salamandra decoders at low resource,
finding a large gap — 14.0 against 33.6 word error
rate at ten hours of Italian — that narrows as data
grows. These results show the decoder substantially affects recognition, but the models compared
differ in scale, tokenizer and pretraining corpus at

Checkpoints are selected on the FLEURS (Conneau et al., 2023) validation split, with two exceptions: Hausa is selected on the WorldSpeech test
split, its FLEURS validation split being impractically large to evaluate repeatedly, and Seselwa —
which FLEURS does not cover — on a held-out
2% split of its WorldSpeech data, sized to match a
FLEURS validation split. Final evaluation uses the
held-out FLEURS test and WorldSpeech test splits.

Experimental Setup

Datasets and languages. We train on WorldSpeech (Asonitis et al., 2026), a corpus of formal speech from parliamentary proceedings, international broadcasts and public-domain audiobooks, excluding clips of 30 seconds or longer.
The study covers twelve languages (Table 1): four
from the Water region (English, French, Spanish,
Indonesian), four from Fire (Hindi, Marathi, Tamil,
Urdu), three from Earth (Amharic, Hausa, Swahili),
and Kreol Seselwa (Seychellois Creole; hereafter
Seselwa), an African language supported by neither
the encoder nor any Tiny Aya decoder, included to

3

Training. Every run trains only the projector
with AdamW at learning rate 1.5 × 10−3 , batch
size 8 with gradient accumulation 64 (effective
batch 512), 1,000 steps, no weight decay, on one
NVIDIA H200 GPU, with CER evaluated every
10 steps (101 evaluations per run). The grid is 12
languages × 4 decoders = 48 runs. Amharic and
Seselwa across all four decoders — eight cells —
were trained twice with different seeds, giving replicate pairs from which we measure the run-to-run
noise floor directly.

283

Baselines and metrics. We compare against
three off-the-shelf models evaluated on the same
test sets: Whisper-Medium (Radford et al., 2023)
— the same model as our encoder, chosen for
its accuracy–speed tradeoff (Ma et al., 2024) —
Voxtral-Mini-3B (Mistral AI, 2025) and Qwen2-

294

Audio-7B (Chu et al., 2024). CER is the primary
metric throughout, and per-language error rates are
never pooled across languages. Paired contrasts use
the Wilcoxon signed-rank test (Wilcoxon, 1945),
a non-parametric paired test over the per-language
differences that does not assume they are normally
distributed.

308

5

Results

309

5.1

Regional matching does not change what
the model hears

the ten African languages sum to 0.1% of the Fire
mix (0.010% apiece), and South Asia sums to 0.3%
of Water against 0.1% each for Hindi and Marathi,
leaving 0.017% apiece for the remaining six.
The effect scales with neither characterisation.
Regressing the per-language effect on the log exposure ratio gives Spearman ρ = −0.39 on the
selection curve and −0.04 on held-out in-domain
data; the strongest association anywhere is Pearson
r = −0.51 (p = 0.11). Even though exposure
varies by a factor of roughly 400 across these languages, recognition accuracy does not follow it.
English is the informative exception to the labels.
It is a Water language, but the Fire mix contains
46.2% English against Water’s 17.0%, so English
is the one language whose matched decoder saw
substantially less of it — and English is also the
single language where the matched decoder does
worst (+7.39 CER). Where the region label and
the actual exposure disagree, the outcome follows
the exposure.

Table 2 reports, for each language, the best CER
of the region-matched decoder against the mean of
the two mismatched regional decoders and against
Global. Across the eleven languages covered by a
Tiny Aya region, the matched decoder is 0.55 CER
better on average, with a 95% bootstrap confidence
interval of [−3.89, 1.99]; a Wilcoxon signed-rank
test — a paired significance test over the eleven perlanguage differences — finds no significant difference (p = 0.97). The matched decoder is favoured
by six of eleven languages. Figure 2 shows the
same picture across whole training trajectories.
Two reference numbers say how to read this
null result. The first is the design’s detection limit:
given how much the per-language differences vary,
a paired test at the usual 5% significance level with
80% power would only reliably detect a true advantage of at least 4.90 CER. The second is training
noise: retraining the same configuration with a different seed changes its best CER by 0.89 on average
(the between-run standard deviation over the eight
replicate pairs). The observed 0.55 CER advantage
is below the detection limit, and smaller than the
difference between two identical runs.
5.2

5.3

The null replicates on held-out test sets

The contrast above is measured on the curve each
checkpoint was selected against. Evaluating all
four decoders on the held-out test sets repeats it on
data no checkpoint was chosen for. On FLEURS
test the matched decoder is 0.67 CER better on average (p = 0.83); on the in-domain WorldSpeech test
it is 1.71 CER worse (p = 0.70). Urdu’s in-domain
test is the one cell where no decoder transcribes
at all (the best of the four sits at 75.3 CER against
36.6 for the next-worst cell); setting it aside leaves
−0.02 CER over ten languages with a minimum
detectable effect of 0.44. Restricting instead to the
seven languages the manipulation actually treats —
English, Spanish, French and Urdu appear in every
regional mix, with exposure ratios of 0.53–2.72
against 20 or more for the rest — gives −0.09 CER
(p = 0.69) against a minimum detectable effect of
0.67 CER.
Two observations complete the picture. The
sign of the effect is not stable across evaluation
sets (−0.55 selection, −0.67 FLEURS, +1.71 indomain), which is what noise looks like. And the
two largest per-language effects are properties of
the read-speech benchmark rather than of the decoder: Tamil favours its matched decoder by 14.70
CER on the selection curve and 19.58 on FLEURS
test, but by +0.02 on its own in-domain test; English moves from +7.39 to +0.19.

Differences in target-language exposure

A null invites the question of whether the treatment was large enough to detect, and the Tiny Aya
report’s own composition tables answer it in two
ways (Table 3). In absolute terms the treatment is
small: the matched variant saw a median of 1.3
percentage points more of the target language than
the mismatched variants did. In relative terms it
is not: the matched variant saw 236 times more
Amharic, Hausa or Swahili than the mismatched
ones, and 204 times more Tamil, with six of eleven
languages above a factor of twenty. The report
rounds shares to one decimal place, so for four languages the mismatched share is below 0.05% and
is recovered from the printed per-region subtotals:

5.4

low small (median +3.7 CER) and the wins above
not: Amharic improves by 102.6 CER in-domain
and 78.7 on FLEURS, Seselwa by 68.9, Hausa by
45.4, Tanzanian Swahili by 32.0.
Seselwa is the cleanest single case, since neither frozen component supports it. Of the three
off-the-shelf models, two fail to produce output on
it at all, and the third reaches 85.18 CER; a trained
projector reaches 16.28. On the held-out test the
four decoder variants land within 0.50 CER of one
another (16.28–16.78), so which regional variant
is paired with it matters as little there as elsewhere:
the projector learns a usable alignment for a language absent from both pretraining corpora.

Training volume predicts overfitting

While the decoder variant does not separate the
runs, the amount of in-language audio does. Table 4 groups the runs by resource tier: the rise in
evaluation loss after its own minimum — overfitting, measured without reference to the training
distribution — falls monotonically from 0.211 in
the very-low tier to 0.003 in the high tier, and the
lowest-resource cells reach their best evaluation
loss roughly a quarter of the way into training. Lowresource cells need early stopping; high-resource
cells barely overfit at all.

5.5

What projector training buys

5.6

Table 5 compares the best trained checkpoint
against the strongest off-the-shelf model on each
held-out test set. Training wins where the off-theshelf models were failing: the benefit tracks how
badly the best baseline was already doing (Spearman ρ = −0.42, p = 0.005 over all 43 evaluated
cells), and splitting at the median baseline CER
of 15.8, the projector wins 12 of the 22 cells at or
above it and 3 of the 21 below, with the losses be-

The gains do not transfer across dialect

WorldSpeech ships several country varieties per language, and the study trained on only some of them,
so each trained checkpoint can also be evaluated
on varieties it never saw (Table 6). On the eighteen
genuinely held-out varieties, the projector beats the
best off-the-shelf model on none, at a median cost
of 4.1 CER — against 9 of 12 wins on the varieties
it trained on. The result should be read alongside

the languages involved: seventeen of the eighteen
held-out varieties belong to English, Spanish and
French, high-resource languages where even the

to transfer. Whether projector gains transfer across

gains are tens of CER, is an open question this data

Tiny Aya decoder over a mismatched one moves
CER by less than the difference between two runs
of the same configuration, and the null replicates
on held-out data with a bound eleven times tighter
than the selection curve’s. What does move the
result is whether a projector is trained at all, and
how much in-language audio is available to train it.

What the negative result does and does not show.
Our result is limited to the differences that exist
within this model family. Those differences are
small in absolute terms (a median of 1.3 percentage
points of post-training data) but large in relative
terms (up to 236 times more target-language data),
and accuracy follows neither. Decoder choice can

Discussion

What a practitioner should take from this. If
the goal is to add a low-resource language to a
speech interface, the decoder variant is not where
the decision lies. Choosing the regionally matched

Table 4: Overfitting by resource tier. Loss rise: how

(larger = more overfitting). Frac. to best: how far into

still matter between model families: Fong et al.
(2025) report large gaps between EuroLLM and
Salamandra. What our result says is that for a
practitioner choosing among the released variants
of one family, the regional label is not the deciding
factor. English, the one language whose label and
exposure disagree, follows the exposure — so if
specialization matters anywhere, it is where the
data difference is tens of percentage points, not
one.

The frozen decoder is doing less work than its
size suggests. Seselwa is supported by neither
frozen component, and the projector still reaches error rates comparable to the low-resource end of the
supported languages, with the four variants within
0.50 CER of one another. Whatever the decoder
contributes, it is not primarily language-specific
knowledge of the target language — consistent
with the region labels making so little difference
everywhere else.

points and transcripts so the comparison can be
extended to other decoder families, where the differences between models are larger — and because
a SLAM-ASR model, unlike a traditional ASR system, can be further instruction-tuned for tasks such
as summarization, question answering or sentiment
analysis, these checkpoints are also groundwork
for extending these low-resource languages beyond
transcription.

Conclusion

We asked whether pairing a language with its
regionally specialized decoder improves speech
recognition in the SLAM-ASR framework. Across
twelve languages and four Tiny Aya decoders, it
does not. The matched decoder is 0.55 CER better on average (p = 0.97), the result repeats on
held-out test sets, and giving a decoder more targetlanguage data — up to 236 times more — does
not improve its recognition of that language. What
does matter is training the projector at all. On
low-resource languages, the trained projector improves over off-the-shelf models by up to 102.6
CER, including on Seselwa, a language neither
frozen component supports. The practical advice
is simple: a general-purpose multilingual decoder
is enough, and the effort is better spent on collecting in-language audio. We will release all check-

Limitations
Most cells are trained once; eight of the 48 grid
runs have a replicate — the two languages retrained
across all four decoders — which is what lets us
state a between-run standard deviation, but the remaining cells carry no direct uncertainty estimate.
All cells share a single conservative learning rate
and seed. Of the twelve languages, ten train on
WorldSpeech and evaluate on FLEURS, so most
reported numbers combine decoder choice with
a domain shift; we report in-domain test results
alongside, but the two are not separable in every
cell. Furthermore, we use the human transcript
for the WorldSpeech dataset, which may not be
completely accurate for all passages in the train-

9

=== PAPER: Model Capacity Dominates Language Resourcing in Multilingual ASR Quantization ===

Model Capacity Dominates Language Resourcing
in Multilingual ASR Quantization

Anonymous Author(s)
Affiliation
Address
email

Abstract
Multilingual Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) accuracy is correlated to how
much pretraining data a language had. A previous study reports that quantization
further compounds the gap between high- and low-resource languages. We show
that this conclusion is a property of how degradation was measured, an absolute
threshold on the error increase and we ask what governs quantization fragility
instead. On 18 ASR and audio-language models, we evaluate the effect of posttraining quantization (HQQ at 8, 6, 4, and 3 bits) against the BFloat16 baseline
across 25 European languages. We find that the amount of pretraining data has
little explanatory power for the quantization penalty, while model capacity is
significantly more important. Across the Whisper scaling ladder, the relative
CER penalty at 4 bits falls from 129.3% for Whisper-T to 2.8% for Whisper-Lv3.
This capacity effect becomes stronger as the bit-width decreases. Overall, model
capacity has a greater impact on quantization robustness than the choice of HQQ
bit-width, particularly for the larger models.

1

Introduction

Whisper-style models [Radford et al., 2023] and audio-language models [Chu et al., 2024, Xu
et al., 2025, Liu et al., 2025] enable ASR for dozens of languages, but quality is highly uneven.
High-resource languages reach single-digit error rates while low-resource languages lag far behind.
Deployment introduces another concern through quantization, raising the possibility that the two
inequalities compound. That concern is well studied in text: 4-bit machine translation and quantized
multilingual LLMs degrade sharply for low-resource and non-Latin-script languages [Marchisio et al.,
2024, Marie and Fujita, 2025]. For speech, Ferraz [2024] reports the same pattern, with low-resource
languages appearing to be the most adversely affected by quantization.
We find that this apparent disparity is a property of the measurement rather than of quantization.
Ferraz [2024] classifies an utterance as degraded when its error rate rises by more than five points,
measured with LLM.int8() [Dettmers et al., 2022] at 8-bit. A fixed absolute threshold flags the
languages with the highest baselines, regardless of what quantization does to them. And baselines
here differ by an order of magnitude depending on the hours of pretraining data a language received,
which we called resourcing. Separating the absolute penalty from the relative one dissolves the effect.
The absolute penalty grows as resourcing falls in proportion to the baseline, while the relative penalty
varies by much less. CER also falls log-linearly with pretraining hours, with Pearson correlations of
-0.60 to -0.73 across the leading models, and every family’s median CER is at least three times higher
in the low tier than in the high tier (Appendix C). This raises a natural question: does quantization
further widen this existing gap?
Our design makes that separation trustworthy. Every quantized evaluation is paired with its own
BFloat16 baseline on the same (model, dataset), so each delta compares one checkpoint against itself

at two precisions. The grid covers 18 models, 25 languages and four bit-widths of Half-Quadratic
Quantization (HQQ) [Badri and Shaji, 2023], a calibration-free post-training method that applies
uniformly across languages. When we ask what predicts the penalty, model capacity within a fixed
architecture emerges as the dominant factor, with compressed checkpoints more fragile than their
size suggests.

50

Two recent studies quantize small ASR models. Edge-ASR [Feng et al., 2025] benchmarks eight
PTQ methods on Whisper and Moonshine checkpoints up to 244M parameters, and Söhler et al.
[2026] compare quantization libraries on Whisper tiny, base and small. Both find that the larger of
these small models withstand low bit-widths better, and both evaluate only English. We extend this
line in two directions: to billion-scale models, and across 25 languages whose resourcing spans three
orders of magnitude. Our contributions are threefold. We separate the absolute from the relative
quantization penalty and show the apparent resourcing effect belongs to the former. We show that
model capacity, not resourcing, is what decides how much a model loses. And we show a compressed
checkpoint breaking the size trend, losing far more than plain models of its size.

51

2

Experimental Setup

Models. 18 ASR and audio-language models spanning four families and three orders of magnitude:
8 Whisper checkpoints [Radford et al., 2023] (including the compressed turbo variant Wh-Lv3t),
6 LiteASR compressions of Whisper-large-v3 [Kamahori et al., 2025], 2 Qwen audio-LLMs [Chu
et al., 2024, Xu et al., 2025] and 2 Voxtral models [Liu et al., 2025], from 37.8M to 24B parameters.
Datasets. 25 European languages of short-form speech (utterances under 30s) from Common
Voice 15 [Ardila et al., 2020], MLS [Pratap et al., 2020] and FLEURS [Conneau et al., 2022], giving
47 evaluation sets (Appendix A). Each language is grouped by its combined Whisper pretraining
hours into four resource tiers: low (<316 h), medium (316–3,162 h), high (3,162–31,623 h) and
extra (≥31,623 h, English only). Table 1 lists each tier’s hour range and its number of evaluation
sets. We report character error rate (CER), which tracks human judgement more closely than WER
for multilingual ASR [K et al., 2025]; scoring with WER changes no conclusion (Appendix D).
Quantization. We use HQQ [Badri and Shaji, 2023] at 8-, 6-, 4- and 3-bit weight-only quantization
with group size 64. All unquantized components use BFloat16, which serves as our BFloat16 baseline.
Quantization covers every linear layer except the output head; the convolutional layers, LayerNorm,
and embeddings remain in BFloat16. Every bit-width covers the same roster, and every run uses
offline batch decoding with BFloat16 activations and greedy decoding. The decoder’s language token
is forced per evaluation set to prevent language identification from affecting the measured deltas.
Since HQQ requires no calibration data, the quantization procedure is applied uniformly across
languages. A run whose CER reaches 200 is treated as failed rather than degraded and is excluded;
results are reported as medians (Appendix B).

76

Statistical analysis. We use the Kruskal–Wallis test [Kruskal and Wallis, 1952] to compare relative
CER penalties across multiple resource tiers or models, and the Mann–Whitney test [Mann and
Whitney, 1947] for direct comparisons between two groups. Both are nonparametric tests based on
the ranks of the observations, avoiding assumptions about normally distributed penalties. We use
p < 0.05 as the threshold for statistical significance.

77

3

Results

78

3.1

Resourcing increases the absolute penalty but barely the relative one

79

Table 1 reports the paired relative CER increase from BFloat16 to each HQQ bit-width, by tier, across
the 16 models. Qwen models are excluded from this analysis (Appendix B). Runs are paired by
(model, dataset): every delta compares a model against itself on the same test set.

The absolute and relative penalties tell different stories. At 4 bits, the absolute CER increase
grows as resourcing falls: 0.151, 0.336, 0.773 for high, medium and low respectively. This reflects
the higher baseline CER in low-resource languages, where the same relative increase translates
into a larger absolute increase. Dividing by each language’s own BFloat16 baseline removes this
effect, with the relative penalty varying by only 1.12× between the high and low tiers. English
(extra) follows the same pattern, with an absolute penalty between those of the high and medium tiers

88

Model

(Appendix C). This also explains why the five-point degradation threshold used by Ferraz [2024] gives
the impression of a strong resource effect. In their analysis, an utterance is considered degraded when
quantization increases its error rate by more than five percentage points. Applied to our 4-bit runs,
the threshold flags 16.8% of low-tier runs as degraded compared with 8.0% of high-tier runs. Using a
relative threshold instead largely removes this difference, flagging 14.4% and 9.3% respectively.

99

Across the pooled runs, we find little evidence that resourcing explains the relative quantization
penalty. The differences between resource tiers are not significant under Kruskal–Wallis p=0.45,
and the direct high-versus-low comparison is similarly inconclusive (Mann–Whitney p=0.19). The
differences between individual models are much larger, which we examine in the next section (Sec.
3.2). The same overall pattern holds across bit-widths, although the 3-bit results show greater
variation than the higher-bit settings. Restricting the analysis to runs with BFloat16 CER below 20
gives the same result, with no significant difference across resource tiers (Kruskal–Wallis p=0.98).

100

3.2

101

Applying the same analysis across models shows a much larger difference than across resource tiers.
At 4 bits, the relative CER penalty ranges from 129.3% for Wh-T to 2.8% for Wh-Lv3. The penalty
falls consistently as model size increases, with the largest reductions occurring at the small end of the
Whisper scaling ladder. This pattern is much stronger than the small differences observed between
resource tiers.

Model capacity strongly affects quantization fragility

Model capacity, measured by parameter count, is the main source of variation in quantization fragility,
but its effect depends on the architecture. Across all 16 models, parameter count has only a weak
correlation with relative penalty (Spearman ρ=−0.28). For example, Voxtral-Mini (4,676M) has a
larger relative penalty of 18.9% from BFloat16 to 4-bit quant than the smaller LiteASR-la (1,338M),
3

whose penalty is only 2.4%. Thus, parameter count alone is not a reliable predictor across
different architectures.
However, within a single architecture the relationship is much clearer. Restricted to the Whisper
models, which form a consistent scaling ladder, relative penalty decreases strongly with parameter
count (Spearman ρ=−0.88), as shown in Figure 1 and Table 2. The penalty falls monotonically
from Wh-T through Wh-L, then flattens across the three 1.5B checkpoints, showing that the benefit
of additional capacity is largest at the smaller end of the ladder and becomes smaller at the
top. Voxtral’s two checkpoints show the same trend (18.9% at 4.7B against 10.8% at 24.3B), while
LiteASR shows why architecture must be held fixed, its variants differ by compression strategy rather
than size, and size barely predicts fragility within it (ρ=−0.18).
The compressed Wh-Lv3t is an important exception. With 809M parameters, it is similar in size to
Wh-M and Wh-L, but its 8.5% relative penalty is 1.9–7.6× larger than that of the plain models of
comparable size. This shows that model size alone does not determine quantization fragility, as the
way a model was trained or compressed can also matter.
The capacity effect becomes stronger as precision falls. The correlation across the Whisper ladder
changes from ρ=−0.15 at 8-bit to -0.31 at 6-bit, -0.70 at 4-bit, and -0.76 at 3-bit. At 8-bit, quantization
adds little to no error, whereas at 3-bit the penalty is strongly associated with model capacity. In
other words, lower bit-widths make capacity increasingly important for quantization robustness,
while resourcing has only a small effect (Sec. 3.1).

135

There are a few caveats to this trend. LiteASR-tf has a very high BFloat16 baseline, making its
0.1% relative penalty look large despite a small absolute increase. The smallest Whisper models also
quantize a smaller fraction of their parameters because the tied embedding and output head remain in
BFloat16, yet they still have the largest penalties. This means the capacity trend is not simply a
result of quantizing more parameters in smaller models. Finally, more 3-bit runs are removed
by the degenerate-run filter than at BFloat16, so the 3-bit results should be interpreted with some
caution (Appendix B).

136

4

Conclusion

Pretraining hours predict BFloat16 CER well, but have only a limited effect on the relative quantization
penalty. At 4 bits, the low-resource tier has a relative penalty of 6.4% compared with 5.7% for the
high tier, while differences between models are much larger. Within the Whisper scaling ladder, the
4-bit penalty falls from 129.3% for Wh-T to 2.8% for Wh-Lv3, with a strong correlation between
model size and penalty (ρ=−0.88). This relationship becomes stronger as precision is reduced,
showing that model capacity becomes increasingly important for quantization robustness at lower
bit-widths. Overall, our results suggest that quantization does not inherently widen the low-resource
gap; the apparent resource effect is largely explained by differences in BFloat16 baseline error, while
model capacity has a much larger influence on quantization fragility.
For deployment, these results favor the largest plain checkpoint that fits within a fixed architecture,
particularly when using lower bit-widths. The compressed Wh-Lv3t is an important exception,
showing substantially higher fragility than plain models of similar size and indicating that model
history also matters beyond parameter count.

157

Our study uses only one PTQ method, HQQ, and focuses on European short-form speech. The
3-bit results are also affected by the removal of degenerate runs. The capacity trend is based mainly
on the Whisper scaling ladder, with only two Voxtral models and one compressed checkpoint as
additional evidence; the LiteASR family varies in compression strategy rather than size, showing that
differences in training and compression can also affect quantization fragility beyond parameter count.
Further evaluation across quantization methods, architectures, languages, and training strategies is
therefore needed to determine how broadly these findings generalize. Appendix F discusses these
limitations in more detail.

158

short-form utterances per full model sweep.

244

Each language is mapped to its Whisper pretraining hours from the original Whisper report [Radford
et al., 2023], combining the ASR (transcription) and X to English translation hours, since both shape
the model’s exposure to the language. Languages are grouped by the order of magnitude of this
combined figure, so a tier spans one power of ten and its boundaries fall halfway between powers of
ten on the log scale: 316 h, 3,162 h and 31,623 h. The extra tier exists because English’s 438,218 h is
≈25× the next-highest language (Spanish, 17,793 h) and would otherwise single-handedly distort the
high tier. The tiers are then well balanced: 9 high (Spanish, German, Russian, French, Portuguese,
Polish, Italian, Dutch, Swedish), 9 medium (Finnish, Greek, Ukrainian, Hungarian, Romanian,
Danish, Czech, Slovenian, Croatian), 6 low (Bulgarian, Slovak, Lithuanian, Latvian, Estonian, and
Maltese at 42.1 h), and English alone in extra.

245

B

Outlier policies

Qwen output-language failure. The Qwen audio models collapse on medium and low-resource
languages regardless of precision (Table 5). Their hypotheses there drift into other languages
(German or English text for Latvian audio) and repetition loops, in BFloat16 as much as quantized.
Whatever its trigger, the failure is independent of precision, so the models are excluded from the
quantization-by-tier analysis.
6

260

Degenerate runs. A run whose CER reaches 200 has failed rather than degraded, so we drop it
and report medians, which are robust to the remaining heavy tail. The filter removes 0.8% of runs
at BFloat16 but 8.2% at 3-bit. Of those 69 collapses, 68 come from the two smallest Whisper
checkpoints and the two most compressed LiteASR variants, and every 3-bit run of Wh-T collapses.
This removal, if anything, understates the capacity effect. The filter also removes more low-tier
than high-tier runs (2.3% against 0.3% at 4-bit; 12.5/11.7% of medium/low against 7.0% at 3-bit),
which could make the tiers look more equal than they are. Two re-tests show it does not: keeping the
collapsed 3-bit runs but capping their CER at 200 instead of deleting them still finds no significant
tier difference (p=0.12), and removing LiteASR-tf, the one model with a degenerate baseline, leaves
the pooled 4-bit test unchanged (p=0.48).

C

The BFloat16 resourcing law

Table 4 shows CER falling with log pretraining hours for all leading models over 25 languages.
Against ASR-only hours, the Pearson correlation (rasr ) ranges from −0.60 to −0.73 and the Spearman
rank correlation (ρasr ) from −0.62 to −0.81; against the combined ASR+translation hours that define
the tiers, the Pearson correlation (rcomb ) is moderately weaker. The law is model-agnostic. Voxtral-S,
Wh-Lv3, its turbo variant and LiteASR-la show comparable slopes.

Table 5 gives BFloat16 median CER by tier and model family. Every family degrades by a factor
of three or more from the high tier to the low tier, and no single family leads everywhere. Voxtral
is best on the high tier (2.1 against Whisper’s 3.1), while Whisper is best on medium and low (6.3
and 10.2 against Voxtral’s 7.5 and 12.1). English is the main exception to this pattern. Although its
438,218 h of pretraining data places it above the high tier in three of the four families, its CER does
not continue the log-linear trend. The relationship therefore holds more clearly below the extra tier.
The gap is a property of the BFloat16 models.

277

D

274
275

Metric and aggregation robustness

K et al. [2025] argue that CER tracks human judgement more closely than WER for multilingual ASR, including for whitespace-delimited scripts, which all 25 languages here use. Scoring
with WER instead changes no conclusion, the run-level agreement between the two is Spearman
ρ=0.97 (n=4,112); the resource-tier ordering is identical (Kendall τ =1.0); the resourcing law holds
(r=−0.76 to −0.71); and the flat penalty survives (Kruskal–Wallis p=0.47). Model ranks move by
at most 4 places (τ =0.80).
7

291

Dataset coverage is uneven as French has four evaluation sets while most low-resource languages
have one, so an aggregate over all sets would weight French four times as heavily. We therefore report
two aggregations and label every table with the one it uses. CERrun treats each model’s evaluation on
one set as one observation and takes the median over that model’s 47 evaluations. CERlang first takes
the median within each language’s sets and then the median over the 25 languages, so every language
counts once. Wh-Lv3 scores CERlang =2.8 and CERrun =2.4. An utterance-weighted micro-average
over all short-form utterances ranks models the same way as CERrun (Kendall τ =0.83 across all 18
models).

292

E

Per-family distributions

Figure 2 shows the distributions behind Table 1, per model family. 8-bit is indistinguishable from
BFloat16 and 6-bit adds only a marginal increase; degradation becomes noticeable at 4-bit and
intensifies at 3-bit, where the smaller and already-compressed models spread widely while Wh-Lv3
and Voxtral-S hold stable medians.

Figure 2: CER of 14 of the 18 models at BFloat16 and HQQ 8/6/4/3-bit, by family. Each family’s
worst members are omitted (Wh-T, Wh-B, LiteASR-t, LiteASR-tf) and y-axes differ per panel; the
green line marks a CER of ten throughout. Parameter counts appear under each model.

F

Extended limitations and ethical considerations

Extending to other PTQ families, such as bnb with LLM.int8() and NF4/QLoRA [bitsandbytes
foundation, 2022, Dettmers et al., 2022, 2023], GPTQ [Frantar et al., 2023], Quanto [Hugging Face,
2024], and to non-European languages is future work. Per-tier sample sizes are uneven. We measure
accuracy (CER) only; latency, memory and energy on device are not evaluated. Feng et al. [2025]
and Söhler et al. [2026] also find int8 quantization near-lossless, matching our 8-bit and 6-bit rows;
neither study includes models above 244M parameters, where we find the penalty stops shrinking.
Resource bins follow Whisper’s approximate hour estimates.
All datasets are publicly released benchmarks under research-use licenses; we introduce no new
speech data or speaker-identifying information. The analysis is intended to support more equitable
deployment of quantized multilingual ASR.
8

=== PAPER: How to Prompt Monolingual SLAM-ASR Models Towards Zero-Shot Multilingual Speech Transcription and Translation ===

1

How to Prompt Monolingual SLAM-ASR Models
Towards Zero-Shot Multilingual
Speech Transcription and Translation
Edwin Arkel Rios, Jocelyn Zaruma, Sneh Sourabh, Girish Ewoorkar, Julian Mack, and Bo-Cheng Lai

Abstract—A linear projector trained on one language’s transcription data, between a frozen speech encoder and a frozen
multilingual decoder, recovers what was said in languages it never
saw, but writes it in the wrong language almost every time. Under
its training prompt, an English-trained projector transcribing
Spanish reaches 0.9% language adherence (LA, the share of
outputs written in the reference’s language). It transcribes into
English following Spanish word order. We show the output
language is set by the prompt. Naming the target language in
the text after the audio, in that language, increases LA to 98.1%
and reduces character error rate (CER) by 74.5%. Monolingually
trained projectors then transcribe and translate across languages
they never saw. Cross-lingual transcription reaches a median
CER of 29.1 over on-language outputs at 91.2% median LA
across 4 projector and 4 speech languages. As a step towards
zero-shot speech translation, the English projector averages 12.0
BLEU from four source languages into six targets. On the
seven FLEURS translation targets shared with the purpose-built
AlignFormer, our model scores 13% higher.
Index Terms—Speech recognition, speech translation, multilingual, speech-LLM, prompting.

I. I NTRODUCTION
LAM-ASR [1] builds a large audio language model
(LALM) by connecting a frozen speech encoder to a
frozen text decoder through a linear projector. Only the projector is trained, on transcription data in one language, usually
one projector per language [2], [3], [4], [5]. The model is
then reported to transcribe the languages its projector saw [6].
However, we observe that given Spanish audio, an Englishtrained projector recovers what was said, but transcribes it
into English following the Spanish grammar and word order
(Fig. 1). The content is there and the language is wrong. This
letter asks whether any inference-time change can make the
model write the language it heard.
The wrong-language failure has been observed before. Kim
et al. [8] name it language adherence (LA) and measure it
on an automatic speech recognition (ASR) system trained on
many languages, where it affects at most 2.6% of utterances.
Mundnich et al. [9] report language adherence of 80–98%

S

at Tranxform AI. J. Zaruma is with Institut national des langues et

Fig. 1. (a) A SLAM-ASR [1] model trained only on English transcription,
given Spanish audio. It recovers what was said and writes it in English
following the Spanish word order: un metodo clave becomes a method key,
not the English a key method. (b) Language adherence (LA), the share of
outputs written in the language of the reference, for different ASR systems:
medians over the transfer grid of Section IV for Qwen2-Audio-7B and the
two prompts; the worst case Kim et al. report on their own system.

on languages their zero-resource system never saw. Marchisio
et al. [10] study the same failure in text-only models. Our
results (Section III) show that a monolingual SLAM-ASR
model under its training prompt writes the wrong language
on almost every input, at 0.9% LA.
Previous work shows that the prompt matters for LALMs.
Burdisso et al. [11] vary prompt wording within one language
and find the choice matters; AlignFormer [12] reaches zeroshot instruction following by replacing the projector and modifying the audio-instruction order at training time. Inspired by
this, we ask whether the prompt alone, with no retraining, can
repair LA. Changing only the prompt increases LA to 98.1%.
The prompt enables cross-lingual transcription, and a step
towards translation, with no multilingual and no translation
training. Our contributions are:
• We document how a monolingual SLAM-ASR model
transcribes spoken content in another language word by
word into its own training language.
• We study the impact of the prompt at inference. The text
after the audio decides the output language; the language
that text is written in matters more than what it says.
Changing only that text raises LA from 0.9% to 98.1%
and cuts CER by 74.5%.
• We validate the finding on transcription across 4 projector
and 4 speech languages.
• We take a step towards zero-shot translation: the English
projector translates from four source languages into six
targets; it scores 13% above AlignFormer on 7 FLEURS
translation targets shared between the two models.

II. S ETUP
A. Model and data
The model has three parts: a frozen Whisper-medium encoder [13], a linear projector, and a frozen decoder. Only the
projector is trained, on single-language transcription data from
WorldSpeech [14]. Training runs 1,000 steps at an effective
batch of 512, with AdamW at a learning rate of 1.5 × 10−3
on a cosine schedule. A run takes a median 15.2 h on one H200
GPU. The decoder is Tiny Aya Global [15]; Table VIII repeats
the recipe with Qwen3-4B [16]. A projector evaluated on the
language it was trained on is the own-language projector.
We study four projector languages — English (EN), Spanish
(ES), French (FR) and Indonesian (ID) — and evaluate zeroshot transcription among all four; the tables name languages
by these ISO codes. Evaluation is done on FLEURS [17] test
sets. A transcription cell pairs a projector language with a
speech language, which gives 12 cells; we call this set the
transfer grid. Speech translation uses our English projector,
given audio in the four source languages, into English (EN),
Spanish (ES), French (FR), Portuguese (PT), Indonesian (ID)
and Chinese (ZH), 20 cells in all; FLEURS records the same
sentences in every language.

B. Metrics
Transcription is scored by corpus character error rate (CER).
Language adherence (LA) is the share of hypotheses whose
language, as read by the fastText identifier [18], matches that
of their own reference (for translation, the target language).
Following Mundnich et al. [9] we also rescore over only
the on-language hypotheses (CER† , BLEU† ). LaBSE [19] is
the cosine between hypothesis and reference sentence embeddings. Translation uses sacreBLEU [20] against FLEURS
parallel references. Every score is computed on normalised
text, lowercased with punctuation and accents removed, and
an empty hypothesis scores a LaBSE cosine of 0.
III. T HE P ROMPT S ETS THE O UTPUT L ANGUAGE
A prompt has two parts: the preamble is the instruction
text before the audio, and the cue is the text after it, which
the model continues. The ablation holds one cell fixed, the
English projector on Spanish FLEURS audio, and varies the
prompt alone, one factor per block of Table I, which gives
every prompt verbatim with its CER, LA and LaBSE.
Naming the target language. Under the training prompt,
the English projector on Spanish audio reaches 67.0 CER at
0.9% LA. Fig. 1 shows what those errors are: the model writes

The language of the cue. Writing that cue in the target
language is the next step, at 22.8 CER. Adding a preamble in
the same language reaches 17.1 at 98.1% LA, and this is the
prompt we call the best prompt from here on.
Instruction language. With the content held fixed and only
the language of the instruction varied, Spanish and French
instructions reach the lowest error, and instructions in English,
Indonesian, Swahili or Hindi are worse than either.
Preamble versus cue. Crossing the preamble language with
the cue language shows the cue after the audio dominating:
a Spanish cue behind an English preamble beats an English
cue behind a Spanish preamble. Prompting improves LA and
reduces CER, but a gap remains to the own-language projector
(3.2 against 17.1 CER).
IV. Z ERO -S HOT C ROSS -L INGUAL T RANSCRIPTION

of the best prompt — naming the speech language but not
the projector’s — works on languages a projector never saw.
Table II evaluates it cell by cell across the transfer grid,
and Table III gives the grid medians under both prompts

from four source languages into six targets, cell by cell. It
averages 12.0 BLEU (13.1 over on-language hypotheses) at
92.6% mean LA over the 20 cells. Several cells stay at singledigit BLEU. The source language orders every target: English
audio is the best source into each of its five targets (14.3–

five (3.6–5.8), with Spanish and French between. Relatedness
orders the cells as well: targets in the source’s script and family
average 13.3 BLEU at 94.8% LA, same-script unrelated targets
12.0 at 92.2%, and Chinese, the one other-script target, 10.5
at 91.3%.
A Whisper-medium→NLLB-200-distilled-600M [21] cascade on identical cells reaches 26.2 BLEU. The gap is expected, because the cascade’s components are trained on translation and on all six targets; what the comparison establishes
is the regime, 12.0 BLEU out of monolingual transcription
training against 26.2 out of a supervised cascade.
The prompt language controls the translation language.
Table V changes the language the translation prompt is written
in. It matters as it did for transcription: Spanish audio into
French reaches 5.9 BLEU at 40.0% LA under a prompt written
in English, and 15.0 at 98.7% under the same prompt written
in French; into Chinese, LA rises from 72.4% to 96.0%.
The projector reaches 63% of the decoder’s translation
quality. Table VI feeds the same frozen decoder text instead
of audio, every parallel sentence of the same language pairs,
and scores its first translation segment on normalised text as
the speech cells are. Given the reference transcript and a cue in
the target language, the decoder reaches 18.9 BLEU at 89.6%
LA. The projector reading audio reaches 12.0, or 63% of what
the decoder reaches from text; the rest is lost between the audio
and the decoder. Under an English cue the same transcripts fall

to 13.6 BLEU and 70.8% LA. The English projector’s own

The cue effect thus appears with reference text and with the

Comparison against previous state-of-the-art. The closest
published system is AlignFormer [12]: a purpose-built projector trained on 38k hours of English transcription behind
a frozen in-house encoder and a frozen 3.8B decoder [22].
Table VII evaluates our model against AlignFormer on the
FLEURS English→X targets AlignFormer reports. Cascade
is the Whisper-medium→NLLB-200-distilled-600M system of
Table IV, extended to German (DE), Italian (IT) and Japanese
(JA); the Text columns give each frozen decoder the reference
transcript instead of the audio, ours through Tiny Aya Global
and theirs through Phi-3.1. Over the seven targets both systems
score, ours averages 17.0 BLEU against AlignFormer’s 15.0,
a gain of 2.0 BLEU or 13%, with 14% fewer parameters
(3.7B against 4.3B) and 16 times less transcription data
(2,700 against 42k hours seen). Against each decoder’s own
reference-transcript ceiling on Spanish, our projector reaches
73% and theirs 59%.
Ablation on the decoder. Table VIII repeats the recipe
with Qwen3-4B as the frozen decoder, on both grids. Qwen34B trails Tiny Aya Global on every column: transcription
adherence reads 70.4% against 91.2% median LA, translation
adherence 85.5% against 92.6% mean LA. The prompt steers
both decoders, so the effect is a property of the recipe, not of
one decoder.
VI. C ONCLUSION
A monolingual SLAM-ASR model recovers the content of
speech in languages it never saw and writes it in its training
language, following the spoken language’s grammar. The text
after the audio, written in the target language, sets which
language the model writes, and the effect belongs to the frozen
decoder. Across the transfer grid, median LA rises from 1.0%
to 91.2% and median CER falls by 46%. As a step towards
zero-shot translation, the English projector reaches six target
languages at 12.0 BLEU without any translation training, 13%
above AlignFormer on the seven shared FLEURS targets.

=== PAPER: BAQET: BRAM-Aware Quantization for Efficient Transformer Inference via a Stream-Based Architecture on an FPGA ===

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON CIRCUITS AND SYSTEMS—II: EXPRESS BRIEFS

1

BAQET: BRAM-Aware Quantization for Efficient Transformer
Inference via a Stream-Based Architecture on an FPGA
Ling-Chi Yang, Chi-Jui Chen, Trung Le, Bo-Cheng Lai, Member, IEEE, Scott Hauck, and Shih-Chieh Hsu

Abstract—High-level synthesis flows such as hls4ml shorten
the path from a trained model to an FPGA, but their fully onchip dataflow architectures spend block RAM (BRAM) faster
than any other resource, and a design that exceeds the budget
fails at place and route. This brief presents BAQET, a streambased Transformer accelerator whose bit-widths are chosen with
BRAM usage in the loop. The architecture implements Flash
Attention with ping-pong buffers, shallow FIFOs and streamof-blocks buffers matched to the lifetime of each intermediate,
which removes the N × N attention matrix and the FFN hidden
activations from on-chip memory. A simulated-annealing search
then assigns a fixed-point width to every variable, scoring each
candidate with a PyTorch fake-quantized model and a BRAM
estimator instead of synthesis. On an Alveo U55C at 200 MHz, the
search reduces BRAM usage from its starting bit-widths by 30%
to 50% on a neural data Transformer for a 0.8% to 1.7% drop
in co-bps (co-smoothing bits per spike), and by 50% on DeiTTiny for 3.85 points of top-5 accuracy. The resulting designs reach
55.2 GOPS/W at the nominal 200 MHz (54.8 at the 198.5 MHz the
design closes), 2.6× to 8.1× the efficiency of prior Transformer
accelerators of different device capacity and model scale, one of
them an ASIC, with every weight on chip.
Index Terms—FPGA, Transformer, high-level synthesis, quantization, dataflow architecture, simulated annealing.

I. I NTRODUCTION

T

RANSFORMER models lead in vision and language
tasks [2], [3], and FPGA implementations that keep
attention and intermediate results on chip reach higher efficiency than GPUs [4]–[7]. Both workloads of this brief are
edge ones: a neural data Transformer that decodes spiking
activity in a closed loop, where latency and power bound
the device, and a small vision Transformer for an embedded
camera. Frameworks such as hls4ml [8] generate such designs
directly from PyTorch or ONNX through high-level synthesis
(HLS), but three problems remain. There is no design-space
exploration (DSE) tool, so parameters are tuned by hand across
slow synthesis iterations. A fully on-chip architecture stores
every weight in BRAM, which limits the models that fit.
BRAM usage is known only after synthesis or place and route,
so an infeasible design is discovered late.
L.-C. Yang and B.-C. Lai are with the Institute of Electronics, National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Hsinchu, Taiwan (e-mail:
Graduate Degree Program of the College of Electrical and Computer Engineering, National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University. T. Le and S. Hauck
are with the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, and S.-C.
Hsu with the Department of Physics, University of Washington, Seattle, WA,
USA.
A one-page abstract of this work appeared at the ACM/SIGDA International
Symposium on Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGA ’25) [1]; this brief
adds the architecture and its buffer analysis, the tile-size sweep, the search
procedure and the two-model evaluation.

This brief targets the BRAM bottleneck directly. Its contributions are:
• A stream-based Flash Attention [9], [10] architecture
for FPGAs that pairs each intermediate with the cheapest buffer that supports its access pattern. For DeiTTiny (DeiT-T) [11] and the neural data Transformer
(NDT) [12], [13] it removes 30% and 23% of the theoretical activation and weight memory of a Transformer
layer.
• An hls4ml extension for arbitrary-precision fixedpoint post-training quantization of Transformers, and a
simulated-annealing (SA) search over per-variable bitwidths whose cost combines estimated BRAM usage with
model accuracy. Relative to the search’s starting widths
(18 bits for NDT; 8-bit weights with 24-, 12- and 18bit intermediates for DeiT-T), it cuts BRAM by 50% on
NDT for a 1.7% loss in co-bps, or by 30% for 0.8%, and
by 50% on DeiT-T for 3.85 points of top-5 accuracy.
• Designs on an Alveo U55C that reach 1564 GOPS and
55.2 GOPS/W, 2.6× to 8.1× the efficiency of prior FPGA
Transformer accelerators.
II. R ELATED W ORK
FPGA Transformer accelerators divide into single
processing-element-array designs that stream weights from
external memory and dataflow designs with one compute
unit per layer that keep data on chip [14]. FTRANS [5]
keeps intermediate results on chip and weights off chip in a
16-bit HLS dataflow. ViA [4] interleaves two reusable HLS
kernels for attention and MLP. SwiftTron [15] implements
integer-only attention, FFN and LayerNorm units in RTL.
SWAT [7] reduces external accesses for Swin Transformers at
16 bits. ViTA [6] prefetches weights column-wise to respect
a BRAM limit. All of them buffer weights or activations
repeatedly to avoid external memory; none chooses bit-widths
against a BRAM budget. On the tool side, H2PIPE [16]
and fpgaConvNet [17] model resources analytically to
prune the search space before synthesis, but target CNNs.
AutoQKeras [18] searches per-layer bit-widths for hls4ml
networks against an energy or bit-count model; its models are
MLPs and CNNs and its cost is not the BRAM the on-chip
Transformer runs out of. BAQET brings a BRAM model into
the quantization search for Transformers.
III. S TREAM -BASED A RCHITECTURE
A. Overall Dataflow
The design follows the hls4ml dataflow template. Inputs and
outputs cross an AXI-Stream interface to HBM; all weights sit

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON CIRCUITS AND SYSTEMS—II: EXPRESS BRIEFS

in on-chip ROM; FIFOs join consecutive layers, and a blocklevel protocol starts, stalls and completes each layer. Every
layer runs concurrently with its predecessor except the QK ⊤
product in multi-head self-attention (MHSA), which waits for
the last token of the preceding linear layer. The residual FIFO
must hold two inputs’ worth of tokens: the add operation waits
for MHSA while LayerNorm already consumes the next input,
and a full residual FIFO deadlocks the pipeline.
B. Flash Attention on the FPGA
On a GPU, Flash Attention tiles Q, K and V to reuse them
from SRAM. On an FPGA the same matrices can be placed
in BRAM once, so the tiling serves a different purpose: it
fixes the parallelism BN × Bdk of each task and lets four
tasks run as a fine-grained pipeline (Fig. 1): the linear layer
for Q, K and V ; the scaled dot product QK ⊤ with the online
softmax; the P V product with output rescaling (P V O); and
the output linear layer. Q, K and V need random access and
use ping-pong (PIPO) buffers of depth two. The unnormalized
probabilities P and the rescaling factor Rf of the online
softmax live only between two tasks and pass through shallow
FIFOs. The P V O task accumulates the outer product P V in a
dual-port RAM, which removes the port dependency between
rows of O, and applies the final division through an inverse
table. O is read sequentially across tokens by the output linear
layer but randomly within a token, so it uses a stream-ofblocks (SoB) buffer of depth two or more. The FFN maps
d → df f n → d with equal work in its two linear layers; a
SoB of depth two between them lets both run concurrently.
For one Transformer layer with N tokens, width d, h heads
and FFN width df f n , the memory of MHSA is 4d2 + 4N d +
hN 2 +2N d (weights, Q, K, V , O, attention, input and output)
and that of the FFN is 2d df f n + N df f n + 2N d. The shallow
FIFOs and SoBs remove hN 2 + N d from MHSA and N df f n
from the FFN. With the shapes in Table II this is 23% of the
total for NDT and 30% for DeiT-T.
C. Tile Size and BRAM Estimation
With TN = N/BN , Tdk = dk /Bdk and Tf f n =
df f n /Bf f n , the four MHSA tasks spend TN Tdk d, TN2 Tdk ,
TN2 Tdk and TN Tdk d cycles per input and the FFN about
TN Tf f n Td . The pipeline stalls unless the tasks match, which
requires Tdk ≈ Tf f n and TN ≈ d; since MHSA uses about
twice the resources of the FFN, tile pairs that stall MHSA are
avoided. For DeiT-T the matching pairs are (Bdk , Bf f n ) =
2n (1, 12). Table I reports the post-route results: each doubling
of the tile halves the interval, DSP and LUT usage grow with
the tile, and BRAM usage falls from (2, 24) to (8, 96). At
(16, 192) the URAM bandwidth no longer suffices, BRAM
compensates, and timing fails at 5 ns by 1.5 ns; (8, 96) misses
the period by 38 ps.
BRAM usage is estimated before synthesis from
the shape (d, w) that HLS assigns to each array.
A RAMB36 primitive takes one of the shapes
(1k, 36), (2k, 18), (4k, 9), (8k, 4), (16k, 2), (32k, 1);
arrays
larger than 36 kbit cascade primitives. For ROM, Vivado fixes
the primitive from the address width and cascades in width;

IV. BRAM-AWARE Q UANTIZATION
Fig. 2 shows the search. A PyTorch model with a fake
quantizer after every matrix product and non-linearity mirrors
the fixed-point arithmetic of the HLS design,
(︁
(︁
)︁)︁
Q(x) = 2−f Clip Round 2f x , f = btotal − bint , (1)
with btotal and bint the total and integer bits of the variable’s
fixed-point type, evaluated in float64 so that any width can be
represented. Calibration sets the integer bits of each quantizer
from the observed range. Two configurations share the bitwidths: the quantization configuration of the PyTorch model
and the hls4ml configuration, which also fixes the parallelism
of each layer (kept outside the search). Fake quantization runs
about 5× faster than CPU evaluation and over 100× faster
than HLS or RTL simulation, which is what makes a search
of thousands of candidates practical.
Each SA step (i) changes the width of one variable by one
or two bits, (ii) loads the widths into both configurations, (iii)
evaluates the energy

(2)

(iv) accepts a worse candidate with probability exp(−∆E/T )
at the current temperature T and otherwise restores the previous state, and (v) lowers T ; T runs from Tmax to Tmin over
the fixed number of trials. The penalty is 1 and becomes 2
above 80% BRAM usage, where place and route becomes hard
to close; α weights accuracy against BRAM and defaults to 1.
The accuracy term is the model’s own metric, co-bps for NDT
and top-5 accuracy for DeiT-T, normalized by accuracybest ,
the accuracy of the starting configuration; both metrics are
higher-is-better, hence the minus sign.

The required HBM bandwidth, about 2N d bytes per interval,

NDT at 18 bits for every variable except 12-bit table inputs,

Tmin = 10−8 . All accuracies are evaluated in software with
the fake-quantized model, and every loss is relative to the
search’s starting configuration. GOPS is the model’s FLOPs
(Table II) divided by the interval.

from step to step and drops sharply at the lowest widths, while
BRAM usage falls steadily to about half of the initial design.

VI. C ONCLUSION
BAQET couples a stream-based Flash Attention architecture, whose buffers are matched to the lifetime of each
intermediate, with a simulated-annealing quantization search
that scores candidates by estimated BRAM usage and software
accuracy. The architecture removes 23% to 30% of a layer’s
theoretical memory; the search removes a further 30% to 50%
of BRAM at a 0.8% to 1.7% cost in co-bps on NDT, and
50% on DeiT-T. Both models run entirely on chip on an
Alveo U55C at up to 55.2 GOPS/W. Supporting models whose
weights exceed on-chip memory, by trading HBM bandwidth
for BRAM, is the next step.
R EFERENCES

Fig. 4. NDT resource utilization and co-bps after SA for different α.

3.85-point loss in top-5 accuracy.

C. Performance and Efficiency
Table III compares the final designs with prior FPGA
Transformer accelerators. DeiT-T uses the tile pairs (4, 48)
and (8, 96) from Table I; NDT uses (7, 16) and (13, 32),
chosen by the same procedure on its own sweep. DeiT-T at
(8, 96) delivers 1564 GOPS at 28.3 W, 55.2 GOPS/W, which
is 2.6× the efficiency of SWAT and 8.1× that of FTRANS.
Table I shows this design 38 ps short of the 5 ns period; at
the 198.5 MHz it achieves, the figures scale to 1552 GOPS
and 54.8 GOPS/W, and neither ratio changes at one decimal.
The prior works’ power is as their authors report it; ours is
the post-route estimate. Three caveats apply. SwiftTron is an
ASIC. The U55C has 2016 RAMB36 against 1366 on the
U50 used by ViA and SWAT. And FTRANS runs a 125Mparameter RoBERTa with weights off chip, whereas BAQET
keeps every weight on chip, which bounds the model size to
a few million parameters on this device; the comparison is
between architectures, not between equal models.

=== PAPER: Enabling Fine-Grained Image Recognition from Scratch with Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation ===

1

Enabling Fine-Grained Image Recognition from
Scratch with Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation
Edwin Arkel Rios† , Fernando Mikael‡ , Oswin Gosal‡ , Femiloye Oyerinde* ,
Hao-Chun Liang† , Augusto Christian Surya‡ , Bo-Cheng Lai† , Min-Chun Hu‡

Abstract—Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) distinguishes sub-categories within a super-category, and the standard
recipe fine-tunes a backbone pretrained on ImageNet at high
resolution. Deployed sensors often supply low-resolution images,
for which such backbones are a poor fit, and the pretraining
requirement constrains task-specific backbone design. This letter
presents Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation (TGDA), a twostage knowledge distillation framework in which a teacher finetuned on the target dataset supplies both part-attention-driven
augmentations and soft supervision to a student trained from
random initialization. Training the student from scratch leaves
its architecture free, and LRNets (LRN), a ResNet family whose
spatial downsampling is rescheduled for low-resolution inputs,
uses that freedom. On Aircraft, Cars and CUB at 128 × 128,
TGDA with a stock ResNet student gains 8 percentage points of
top-1 accuracy over the strongest prior low-resolution method,
as the mean over four backbone pairs and the three datasets.
The matched LRNet raises that gain to 11 percentage points at
up to 3.9 times fewer parameters. The teacher is pretrained on
ImageNet-1k; the deployed student never sees ImageNet.
Index Terms—fine-grained image recognition, knowledge distillation, data augmentation, efficient architectures

I. I NTRODUCTION
Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) distinguishes subcategories within a super-category, such as bird species [1],
car models [2], or aircraft variants [3], with applications
in biodiversity monitoring, inspection, and agriculture [4].
Standard FGIR methods fine-tune a backbone pretrained on
ImageNet-1k [5] at high resolution. FGIR is difficult because
differences between sub-categories are small while variance
within a sub-category is large, so a model must resolve subtle
part-level cues rather than coarse category shape. Deployed
sensors often deliver low-resolution images, and a backbone
pretrained for high-resolution coarse recognition loses the fine
detail that fine-grained recognition needs. Backbone choice
and size drive FGIR accuracy [6], [7], and the requirement to
pretrain on a large-scale dataset forecloses backbone designs
tailored to the deployment setting.
Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation (TGDA) distills from a
teacher that is itself pretrained on ImageNet-1k for 90 epochs
and then fine-tuned on the target set with Counterfactual Attention Learning (CAL) [8] for 50 epochs. What is trained from
scratch is the deployed student, which never sees ImageNet.
Across the whole pipeline, 120 million images are processed
This work was supported by the National Science and Technology Council,
Taiwan, under grants NSTC 113-2640-E-A49-005, NSTC 114-2218-E-007019, and NSTC 114-2218-E-007-002.
† National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Taiwan. ‡ National Tsing Hua
University, Taiwan. * Cohere Labs Community, Nigeria.

for CUB, of which 96 percent belong to the teacher; the
student’s own share is 4.8 million images. The pretraining
cost is paid once, off-device, by a model that is discarded
once the student is trained; the recurring cost of deployment
is the student’s alone.
Prior knowledge distillation (KD) for FGIR with pretrained
backbones follows an online one-stage co-training scheme,
where CEKD [9] and DSSD [10] share a backbone between
teacher and student and pretrain both. This does not apply
when the student starts from random initialization, since
neither teacher nor student holds pretrained weights to cotrain from. DKD [11] and DADKD [12] distill for lowresolution FGIR under the same pretrained-backbone setup.
Marrie et al. [13] study which pretrained teachers transfer
well when a task-specific teacher is distilled into a smaller
student, so task-specific distillation from a fine-tuned teacher
is established. TGDA follows this line and differs in that its
augmentations are drawn from the teacher’s part attention and
its student starts from random initialization. Sunkara and Luo
[14] replace strided convolutions and pooling with a space-todepth block to preserve detail for low-resolution images and
small objects. LRNets (LRN) share that goal and differ in
keeping the standard residual block while rescheduling when
downsampling happens across stages. Other work trains FGIR
models from scratch without distillation [15], [16].
TGDA with a stock ResNet student gains 8 percentage
points, and with an LRNet student 11 percentage points, as
the mean over the four backbone pairs and the three datasets,
against the strongest prior low-resolution method (Tab. II).
The largest single-cell gain reaches 23 percentage points, and
LRNets use up to 3.9 times fewer parameters than the ResNet
they are paired with, at higher accuracy. This letter makes
three contributions. The first is a two-stage KD framework
that trains FGIR students from scratch under a teacher that
is fine-tuned once and then discarded. The second is LRNets,
a family of ResNet variants that reschedule downsampling to
preserve early spatial detail for low-resolution FGIR. The third
is state-of-the-art low-resolution accuracy at reduced inference
cost on Aircraft, Cars and CUB (Sec. III).
II. M ETHOD
A. TGDA
Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation (TGDA) is a twostage knowledge distillation (KD) framework that trains a
fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) student from random
initialization under the supervision of a specialized teacher.

2

Generic augmentation is not tailored to the discriminative
regions a fine-grained model must resolve; TGDA instead
uses the teacher to generate augmentations grounded in those
regions.
Stage 1: teacher training. A pretrained backbone is finetuned on the target dataset with a fine-grained-aware method
such as WS-DAN [17] or CAL [8]. These methods add a Part
Attention Module (PAM) on top of the encoder to encourage
attention to complementary object parts rather than only the
single most discriminative region. An image Iog is encoded
to feature maps FM, a convolutional block maps these to
N attention maps A, and bilinear pooling [18] of FM and
A gives part-aware feature vectors that a linear layer maps
to predictions. The attention maps A also drive two dataaware augmentations: attention cropping, which zooms into
the highlighted regions, and attention dropping, which masks
them. The teacher is trained with cross-entropy on the original
image and its augmented views, yielding a teacher that produces diverse, discriminative augmentations while remaining
robust to the transformations it generates.
Stage 2: student training. Standard KD [19] trains a
student to match a fixed teacher on a shared input; TGDA
additionally uses the teacher to generate the input. The teacher
is frozen and supervises a student trained from random initialization. Given Iog , the teacher produces augmented views
T
T
S
S
Iaug and predictions yog
, yaug
; the student produces yog
, yaug
from the same inputs. Cropping and masking can make the
ground-truth label ygt ambiguous for an augmented view, so
an augmented view carries only the KD term; the original
image carries both cross-entropy and KD:
[︁
]︁
S
S

S
T
L = LCE (yog
, ygt ) + β LKD (yog

) + LKD (yaug

) ,
(1)
where β weights the teacher supervision. This exposes the
student to a wider range of fine-grained variation than generic
augmentation while keeping supervision consistent through
the teacher. Each training step forwards the teacher once
on the original image and once per augmented view, so the
augmentation cost is a teacher forward pass per view and adds
no student capacity.
B. LRNets
Prior work adapts backbones pretrained for high-resolution
coarse recognition to low-resolution (LR) inputs without
changing their downsampling schedule. LRNets (LRN) instead reschedule the spatial downsampling of the standard
ResNet (RN) [20] to preserve detail under LR inputs. The
downsampling after the stem drops from 4× to 2×, the first
residual stage preserves resolution, downsampling resumes at
the second stage, and an extra stage restores the overall 32×
reduction (Tab. I). This delays early spatial-resolution loss,
letting the network retain fine-grained cues that distinguish
subtle inter-class differences under LR inputs. Channel widths
and block counts are reduced at each stage to keep the
computational cost comparable to RN. Sunkara and Luo [14]
target the same low-resolution regime by replacing strided
convolutions and pooling with a space-to-depth block; LRN

instead reschedules where downsampling occurs and keeps the
standard residual block.
We define four variants over the block type and the per-stage
block-count tuple (L1 , . . . , L5 ). LRN14 and LRN18 use the
basic block, with tuples (1, 2, 1, 1, 1) and (2, 2, 2, 1, 1). LRN26
and LRN50 use the bottleneck block, with tuples (2, 2, 2, 1, 1)
and (3, 3, 3, 4, 3). LRN is a downsampling reschedule of RN,
offered as a proof of concept for task-specific backbone design
under low-resolution FGIR rather than a new architecture
family.

III. E XPERIMENTS
Setup. We evaluate on three fine-grained benchmarks, Aircraft [3], Cars [2] and CUB [1], with the student at 128 × 128
(low resolution, LR) and the teacher at 448 × 448, and report
top-1 accuracy. Students train from scratch for 800 epochs
with AdamW [21], without any ImageNet pretraining. ResNet101 teachers are ImageNet-1k pretrained and fine-tuned on the
target dataset for 50 epochs with SGD using CAL [8], which
learns the part attention maps that drive the augmentations
described in Sec. II. Generic augmentation applies random
cropping, horizontal flipping and TrivialAugment [22] on top
of the data-aware augmentation; regularization applies label
smoothing [23] and stochastic depth [24]. The distillation loss
weight is nominally 10, divided by the squared temperature to
an effective weight of 0.204, and the temperature is 7. Each
cell is one run at one seed; the best result per column is in
bold.
Low-resolution results, decomposed. How much of the
low-resolution gain comes from the TGDA training framework, and how much from the LRNet backbone? Tab. II separates the two: the TGDA row pairs the framework with a stock
ResNet student, and the TGDA* row pairs it with the matched
LRNet student, across the RN18, RN34, RN50 and RN101
capacities on Aircraft, Cars and CUB. Averaged over these
backbone pairs and datasets, TGDA with a stock ResNet gains
8 percentage points over the strongest prior low-resolution
method. Swapping in the LRNet raises the average gain to 11
percentage points, with the largest single-cell gain reaching
23 percentage points. Most of the improvement comes from
the training framework, and the backbone reschedule adds a
further, smaller gain. This indicates the TGDA recipe itself,
not the choice of backbone, is what closes most of the gap to

raises the cost of the early stages, and narrower channels and

matched LRNet the gain is 11 percentage points, both as
the mean over four backbone pairs and three datasets. These
results support training from scratch as a workable route to
backbones designed for the deployment resolution, rather than
adapted from ImageNet-pretrained ones.
The teacher is ImageNet-1k pretrained and then fine-tuned
on the target dataset, so the pipeline as a whole still depends
on large-scale pretraining. 96 percent of the 120 million
images the pipeline processes for CUB are the teacher’s. What
is trained from scratch is the deployed student. The paper
does not measure a teacher trained from scratch. LRNets are
evaluated by floating-point operations (FLOPs), parameters,
throughput and memory on one GPU. No LRNet was measured on an embedded or mobile device, so the letter makes no
claim about on-device latency or energy. Every cell is a single
run at one seed, so differences of a fraction of a percentage
point are not resolved, and no confidence interval is reported.
Hyperparameters were selected on CUB and reused for the
other two datasets.
