Ablations. Which parts of TGDA are necessary, and how
sensitive is it to the augmentation it uses? Tab. IV reports
three blocks, all with a 128 × 128 student and a ResNet-101
teacher. In the components block, only the full configuration,
with a Part Attention Module (PAM) and two-stage training,
reaches the accuracy the other tables report. The block is not a
clean factorial. Its two-stage row keeps the teacher frozen but
augments from class activation maps [25] instead of PAM,
and it diverges. Its two one-stage rows also augment from
class activation maps and differ in the teacher’s own loss,
cross-entropy against cross-entropy plus reverse distillation as
in CEKD [9]; both train stably and plateau well below the
full configuration. In the augmentation-type block, attention
cropping and attention dropping each help less alone than in
combination, so the two augmentations supply complementary
supervision rather than redundant views. In the augmentationmap block, accuracy rises monotonically from 1 to 8 maps on
Aircraft, by 4.4 percentage points. The trend is monotone on
CUB but not on Cars, so the benefit of more maps is dataset
dependent rather than uniform. Fig. 1 shows the PAM maps
localizing different object parts, while the class activation map
highlights a single region.
IV. C ONCLUSION
TGDA trains a fine-grained image recognition student from
scratch by distilling from a task-fine-tuned teacher whose
part attention drives the augmentations, and LRNets reschedule ResNet’s spatial downsampling for low-resolution inputs.
Across Aircraft, Cars and CUB at 128 × 128, the framework gains 8 percentage points over the strongest prior lowresolution method with a stock ResNet student. With the

=== PAPER: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in Fine-Grained Image Recognition ===

Date of publication xxxx 00, 0000, date of current version xxxx 00, 0000.
Digital Object Identifier 10.1109/ACCESS.2026.0000000

A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between
Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in
Fine-Grained Image Recognition
EDWIN ARKEL RIOS1 , AUGUSTO CHRISTIAN SURYA2 , OSWIN GOSAL2 , FERNANDO MIKAEL2 ,
MARY MADELINE NICOLE2 , KISOON JANG2 , BO-CHENG LAI1 , AND MIN-CHUN HU2
1
2

National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Hsinchu, Taiwan
National Tsing Hua University, Hsinchu, Taiwan

ABSTRACT Prior work on fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) has established the importance of
the backbone selection, but has neglected the accuracy-vs-cost trade-offs under different training and
evaluation settings. In this work we conduct a large-scale study with 2,229 experiments across 6 training
and evaluation settings (TrEvS), 16 pretrained backbones, and 17 datasets (9 backbones evaluated on all 17
datasets, with 7 additional backbones evaluated on 4 representative datasets). Preliminary observations on
the effectiveness of data augmentation for fine-grained training motivate us to extend counterfactual attention
learning (CAL), a state-of-the-art method based on data-aware cropping and masking augmentations, with
cross-image discriminative region mixing augmentation (CALMix). Although the counterfactual objective
itself adds no inference cost, CAL—like many FGIR methods—is deployed with a second forward pass
on discriminative crops at evaluation time. Evaluating the same trained models without this second pass,
we find that data-aware augmentation during training alone retains most of the accuracy at a small but
systematic cost of 0.64 points, while significantly reducing inference cost, indicating that the benefit
of such methods is driven by the data-aware training recipe rather than by inference-time cropping. To
support future research we share our code and 825 pretrained checkpoints, loadable as a drop-in library,
at: https://github.com/arkel23/FGIR-Backbones
INDEX TERMS Backbone architectures, benchmark, data augmentation, fine-grained image recognition,
representation learning, transfer learning.
I. INTRODUCTION

Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) tasks—such as distinguishing bird species or car models—require identifying
subtle visual differences between closely related categories.
While previous works on FGIR focused on modules for
discriminative feature selection [1]–[4], recent studies [5]
highlight that the choice of backbone architecture is a critical
decision in FGIR; the backbone not only dictates downstream
accuracy, but is a major factor in training and deployment
costs [5] as our experiments show in Fig. 3.
However, performance is also influenced by the choice of
training and evaluation settings (TrEvS). Prior work [5]–[8]
primarily benchmarks backbones under generic recognition
settings, overlooking the impact of FGIR-specific strategies.
Therefore, in this work, we systematically study the interactions of different TrEvS—including both generic, frozen or
fine-tuned settings and FGIR-specific configurations—on 16
VOLUME 11, 2023

pretrained backbones spanning CNNs and Transformers—
9 evaluated on all 17 fine-grained datasets and 7 additional
backbones on 4 representative datasets, for 2,229 experiments in total—across datasets with diverse domains and data
densities. Our results show that FGIR-specific TrEvS can
improve accuracy by up to 78% relative to a frozen backbone
(CALMix), but can increase training time to roughly 17×
the frozen baseline and reduce inference throughput by up
to 86%. Based on this observation we take counterfactual
attention learning (CAL) [4], a state-of-the-art data-aware
training method, as a case study. We extend its training-time
augmentation with cross-image discriminative region mixing
(CALMix), and we disentangle the contribution of training
from that of the two-pass, attention-cropped inference its implementation shares with many FGIR methods by evaluating
the same trained models without inference-time crops (CALNC and CALMix-NC). The no-crop evaluations show that the
1

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

accuracy benefit stems primarily from data-aware training, so
deployment cost can be reduced substantially without giving
it up.
Our contribution includes:
• We conduct a systematic study of 2,229 experiments—covering 6 training and evaluation settings, 16
backbones (9 evaluated on all 17 datasets, 7 additional
on 4 representative datasets), and 17 datasets—on the
interaction between backbone and training and evaluation settings. Our results provide practical guidelines for
FGIR system design, highlighting that increasing setting
complexity can improve accuracy by up to 78% in relative terms, while increasing training time to roughly 17×
the frozen baseline and reducing inference throughput
by up to 86%.
• We complement the accuracy–cost analysis with a representational analysis based on centered kernel alignment (CKA) between network stages, showing that the
degree of inter-stage feature differentiation strongly predicts fine-grained accuracy and reflects, at the representation level, the effect of dataset difficulty (RIC) and
backbone choice.
• Using CAL as a case study, we extend its training with cross-image discriminative region mixing
(CALMix), a composition of CAL with existing mixing augmentations, and disentangle training-time benefits from inference-time attention cropping by evaluating the same trained models without crops (CALNC, CALMix-NC). These evaluations show that FGIRspecific gains are driven primarily by data-aware training rather than by two-pass inference.
• We release the training, evaluation, and benchmarking
code together with 825 pretrained checkpoints—
one per dataset, backbone, training strategy
and input resolution—packaged as a dropin library, so loading any of them is a single
call:
create_backbone(
’resnet101’,
dataset=’cub’, strategy=’cal’).

scriptors and global semantics; Wei et al. [15] survey the field.
Most of these methods center on locating and re-processing
discriminative regions, and the multi-stage pipelines that do
so at inference significantly increase deployment cost—the
cost our study quantifies.
B. BACKBONE AND BENCHMARK STUDIES

Recent works have explored the impact of backbone architectures on transfer learning and FGIR. Kornblith et al. [6]
analyzed whether higher ImageNet accuracy leads to better
downstream performance, while Goldblum et al. [7] and Jeevan et al. [8] focused on the impact of backbone in general image classification. Their works demonstrated that the
backbone can make as much of a difference as a specialized
discriminative part selection method. Ye et al. [5] investigated
backbones for weakly supervised FGIR and observed that
CNN backbones tend to perform better on structured tasks
such as aircraft and car classification. However, the relative
performance between CNNs and Vision Transformers can
vary depending on training configurations and optimization
choices. These studies benchmark under generic settings;
none of them measures how FGIR-specific training strategies
shift the accuracy–cost trade-off, which is the gap this work
addresses.
C. DATA AUGMENTATION AND REPRESENTATION
ANALYSIS

II. RELATED WORKS
A. FINE-GRAINED IMAGE RECOGNITION (FGIR)

Cross-image mixing augmentations combine regions of two
training images: CutMix [16] pastes random crops across
images, while SnapMix [17], intra-class part swapping [18],
and S3Mix [19] make the mixing semantically aware, which
matters in fine-grained settings where a random patch can
destroy the discriminative cue. CAL [4] instead uses its attention maps for data-aware cropping and masking; CALMix
combines the two lines by mixing attention-selected discriminative regions within a class. To analyze what the trained
networks learn, we use centered kernel alignment [20], a similarity index between layer representations previously used to
compare vision architectures, which to our knowledge has not
been applied at this scale to FGIR.

Early FGIR methods relied on part annotations or bounding
boxes to guide localization [1]. While effective, these methods are difficult to scale due to the cost of annotations. Later
works leveraged image-level labels with attention mechanisms, such as MA-CNN [2], which learned part-aware features without extra supervision, and WS-DAN [3], which uses
its attention maps both to augment training data and to crop
discriminative regions at inference; CAL [4] adds counterfactual supervision on top of this pipeline. Transformer-based
methods select discriminative tokens or regions in a similar
spirit [9]–[11]. Recent methods continue this line: CMALNet [12] treats network layers as mutually teaching experts
whose attention regions serve as augmentation, HERBS [13]
refines multi-scale features while suppressing background,
and CSQA-Net [14] models interactions between part de-

Our experimental protocol consists of two stages. In the
first stage, we perform a learning-rate search per backbone,
dataset, and setting—over {0.3, 0.1, 0.03, 0.01, 0.003} for
the frozen setting and {0.03, 0.01, 0.003, 0.001} for the finetuned settings—selecting the best learning rate based on performance on a held-out evaluation subset of the training data,
and use it for all subsequent experiments. Each backbone
therefore competes at its own best learning rate, so backbone
comparisons are not confounded by a shared training recipe.
In the second stage, we conduct multi-seed experiments using
the selected learning rate for each dataset to account for
stochasticity in deep learning optimization and obtain more
robust performance estimates.

2

A. DATASETS

We evaluate our approach on 17 fine-grained datasets spanning diverse domains and scales, as summarized in Table 1.
We first conduct controlled experiments on 4 representative
datasets. We then extend our evaluation to a broader set of
13 additional datasets for comprehensive analysis. Example
images of each dataset are shown in Figure 1.
B. MODELS

Our experiments consider 16 backbone architectures spanning both CNNs and Transformers, summarized in Table 2.
We begin with a core set of 9 backbones evaluated on all
17 datasets. To cover a wider range of architectures, pretraining schemes, and model scales, we then add 7 additional
backbones for extended experiments on the 4 representative
datasets. Unless otherwise noted, analyses spanning all 17
datasets use the 9 core backbones, while analyses restricted
to the 4 representative datasets use all 16.
C. TRAINING & EVALUATION SETTINGS

Prior work [5], [6], [8] typically evaluates backbones under
fixed configurations, namely Frozen (FZ), where only the
classifier head is trained while the backbone remains fixed,
and Fine-Tuned (FT), where all backbone parameters are
updated on the target dataset. However, they neglected FGIRspecific training strategies that can affect performance. To adVOLUME 11, 2023

dress this, we incorporate Counterfactual Attention Learning (CAL) [4], which improves attention by leveraging bilinear attention pooling (BAP) and counterfactual supervision to
compare factual and counterfactual predictions, encouraging
the model to focus on more discriminative regions.
D. DISENTANGLING DATA-AWARE TRAINING FROM
INFERENCE-TIME CROPPING

The counterfactual objective of CAL adds no computation
at inference [4]; its deployment cost instead comes from the
two-stage inference pipeline its implementation inherits from
attention-cropping methods [3], and which many FGIR methods share [1], [2], [9], [11]: an attention-like module—BAP in
the case of CAL—first predicts discriminative regions from
a global forward pass, and the image is then cropped for a
second local forward pass. To separate the impact of these
inference attention-crops from the data-aware training, we
evaluate the CAL-trained models with the attention cropping
at inference removed. We refer to this variant as CAL-NC
(CAL No Crops); it is an evaluation choice rather than a new
method, reusing the trained weights unchanged. As shown
in Section IV-E, CAL-NC reduces inference cost at a small
but statistically significant accuracy cost, which we quantify
there. These results suggest that the primary benefit of CAL
comes from its data-aware training mechanism. Building on
this observation, we explore whether stronger data-aware
3

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

augmentation can further improve performance. Specifically,
in addition to attention cropping and masking, we incorporate cross-image mixing [16]–[19] by swapping discriminative regions of two images from the same class; we denote
this extended training strategy as CALMix. CALMix is a
composition of CAL’s attention-guided augmentations with
existing cross-image mixing strategies, and its justification
is empirical: it improves robustness by encouraging regionlevel feature learning. Its no-crop evaluation counterpart is
CALMix-NC.
E. EVALUATION METRICS

We evaluate performance and cost using the following metrics:
• Top-1 Accuracy. The percentage of samples where the
predicted class matches the ground-truth label.
• Training Time. The total time required to complete a
full training run, measured in minutes.
• Batched Test Throughput. The number of samples
processed per second during inference, computed under batched evaluation, as opposed to single-sample.
Throughput is measured for the frozen, fine-tuned and
CAL settings; because CALMix alters training-time
augmentation only and leaves the inference graph identical to CAL’s, its throughput is taken from the corresponding CAL measurement rather than measured separately.
• Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) [20]. CKA measures representational similarity between network layers
and is defined as
vec(X T X ) · vec(Y T Y )
.
CKAlinear (X , Y ) =
∥X T X ∥F ∥Y T Y ∥F
We use linear CKA to analyze the learned representations of the trained backbones (Section IV-G).
To enable fair comparison across heterogeneous scales, all
reported values are normalized using min-max scaling before
visualization:
x − min(x)
x′ =
max(x) − min(x)
This allows consistent visualization of the accuracy–efficiency
trade-off within a unified plot.

IV. RESULTS

Before presenting the analyses, we first summarize the evaluation setup and aggregation strategy. The Dataset groups,
Backbone, Image Size, and Training & Evaluation Setting
analyses (Sections IV-A, IV-B, IV-D and IV-E) aim to study
the interaction between model design choices and the performance–cost trade-off, where higher performance generally comes with increased computational cost. The Backbone
analysis is conducted across all 17 datasets, while the Image
Size and Setting analyses are evaluated on the 4 original
datasets. Across the three later analyses, we report min-max
normalized values of accuracy, training time, and inference
throughput to ensure fair comparison across heterogeneous
scales.
A. EFFECT OF BACKBONE ON DIFFERENT DATASET

To analyze the effect of backbone choice across datasets, we
focus on the CAL-NC setting, which provides a favorable
accuracy–cost trade-off. We characterize dataset difficulty
with the Ratio of Images to Classes (RIC), defined as RIC =
Ntrain /C 2 , where Ntrain is the number of training images and
C the number of classes. RIC is close to 1 for ImageNet (1.28)
and decreases for datasets with fewer images, fewer images
per class, or more classes, so a lower RIC indicates a harder
transfer regime. The squared denominator is what makes
RIC a good difficulty descriptor: across the 17 datasets, perdataset mean accuracy rank-correlates with RIC at Spearman
ρ = 0.68–0.79 depending on the setting, compared with
ρ = 0.52–0.60 for images per class (Ntrain /C), while class
count and training-set size alone are weak or non-significant.
Writing RIC = (Ntrain /C) · (1/C) shows why: difficulty
compounds when fewer images per class meet finer class
granularity. The datasets are grouped by RIC order of
magnitude, ranging from -3 (hardest) to 1. RIC establishes
VOLUME 11, 2023

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

values. Throughput is grouped under Cost but higher is better. Costs measured on a single RTX 3090 GPU.

a meaningful difficulty grouping: accuracy increases monotonically with the RIC group from -3 through 0, whether
measured over all 9 core backbones (29% to 92% mean) or
over the best backbone per group as plotted in Figure 2; the
VOLUME 11, 2023

single group-1 dataset (Food-101) performs comparably to
group 0.
Within each difficulty regime, the performance of the backbones also varies. Swin-B leads in the most challenging
5

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

regimes (RIC groups -3 and -2): by roughly 11 points over
the next-best backbone on the single group -3 dataset (SoyGlobal), and by roughly 3 points averaged over the three
group -2 datasets, while ConvNeXt-B leads in the moderate
regime (RIC -1). In the easiest regimes (0 and 1) the top backbones lie within half a point of each other (VAN B-3 narrowly
best at 0; Swin-B and ConvNeXt-B effectively tied at 1),
so backbone choice matters most as dataset difficulty increases. The hierarchical window-based attention mechanism
of Swin-B appears better suited for capturing subtle interclass variations in highly challenging fine-grained scenarios,
whereas on datasets with more separable discriminative cues
most modern backbones perform comparably.
Overall, these findings demonstrate that architectural robustness in FGIR is dataset-dependent rather than universally consistent across benchmarks. As a result, dataset difficulty should be considered during FGIR system design,
since different backbone families may show different behavior under varying fine-grained complexity regimes. RIC provides a practical analytical tool for characterizing dataset
difficulty and guiding backbone selection prior to extensive
experimentation.
B. PERFORMANCE & COST OF DIFFERENT BACKBONES

We next analyze the relationship between model complexity
and computational efficiency across backbone architectures.
Table 2 reports the number of parameters and computational
cost (GMACs) for each model, while Fig. 3 visualizes their
training and inference efficiency.
As shown in Table 2 and Figure 3, more modern and
complex backbone architectures generally incur higher
computational cost, resulting in longer training time and
lower inference throughput. Classical CNN-based models
such as ResNet-101 exhibit lower computational cost and
faster training and inference compared to Transformer-based
models such as ViT-B/16, which are more computationally
demanding, though not every classical CNN does: VGG19 (19.65 GMACs) exceeds ViT B-16 (17.57). While more
complex architectures often achieve stronger performance,
the relationship between complexity and accuracy is not
strictly monotonic, and some highly complex models fail
to provide gains that justify their additional cost, such as
ResNet-101x3 (BiT-M). Among all evaluated backbones,
Swin-B and ConvNeXt-B achieve the best accuracy–cost
trade-off (Figure 3, Table 3), generally outperforming both
lighter CNNs and heavier Transformer variants while maintaining moderate computational overhead, though not on every dataset: in Table 3, DeiT3-L leads SoyGene and Swin-L
leads SoyLocal.
C. EFFECT OF PRETRAINING AND MODEL SCALING

Beyond architectural differences, we further analyze the impact of pretraining data scale and model size on FGIR performance using Table 3. Based on ConvNeXt and Swin models,
increasing pretraining scale from IN1k to IN21k consistently improves accuracy, particularly on more challenging
6

datasets, where the largest gains are 7 points on SoyGene
(Swin-B) and 21 points on SoyLocal (ConvNeXt-B). The
trend does not extend to every family: DeiT3 B-16 drops from
72.31 to 54.10 on SoyGene when its pretraining switches
from IN1k to IN21k. In contrast, increasing model capacity from Base to Large yields marginal and inconsistent
changes for ConvNeXt and Swin, within 1.5 points either
way and not uniformly positive. DeiT3 is the exception:
scaling from Base to Large gains 1.5–2.3 points on three
datasets and 19.5 points on SoyGene, where the Base variant
is anomalously weak (54.10). Capacity therefore helps when
the smaller variant fails to fit the domain at all, but not
otherwise. Overall, these results indicate that pretraining
scale is a more influential factor than model size in FGIR
performance, highlighting the importance of data-centric
rather than parameter-centric scaling strategies.
D. PERFORMANCE & COST OF DIFFERENT IMAGE SIZES

As illustrated in Figure 4, increasing the image resolution from 224 to 384 generally improves accuracy but increases computational cost significantly. This comparison
covers the four settings evaluated at both resolutions (FZ,
FT, CAL, and CAL-NC); CALMix was trained only at 224
on the representative datasets, so it is outside the resolution
sweep. For the three Swin variants, the 3842 runs load the
corresponding window-12, 3842 -pretrained checkpoints, so
window size and pretraining resolution change together with
the input size; all other backbones reuse the same pretrained
weights at both resolutions. Shown in Figure 5, on average,
higher resolution increases training time by up to 143% while
reducing inference throughput by up to 65%, yet yields only
modest accuracy improvements. In extreme cases, training
time increases can exceed 200%. These results indicate that
accuracy gains from higher resolution are not proportional
to the additional computational cost, suggesting that aggressive resolution scaling is unlikely to be a computationally
efficient strategy for improving FGIR systems.
E. PERFORMANCE & COST OF DIFFERENT SETTINGS

As illustrated in Figures 7 and 6, increasing training and
evaluation complexity generally improves FGIR accuracy,
but at significant computational cost. Using the Frozen (FZ)
setting as the baseline, the largest gains come from CALMix:
averaged over the 4 representative datasets and all 16 backbones, its relative accuracy increase is 78%, at the cost of a
1632% relative increase in training time (roughly 17× the
FZ training time). Furthermore, cropping during inference
reduces efficiency, with CAL and CALMix decreasing inference throughput by up to 86% relative to FZ.
To address the inference issue, we evaluate the same
trained models without inference-time cropping (CAL-NC
and CALMix-NC). These variants restore inference efficiency to levels comparable with FZ while retaining most
of the accuracy gain: a mean relative increase of 76% for
CALMix-NC and 60% for CAL-NC. The crops are not free,
and we quantify what removing them costs rather than asVOLUME 11, 2023

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

Cost but higher is better; the CALMix and CALMix-NC throughputs are taken from the corresponding CAL measurements (identical inference graph). Costs
measured on a single RTX 3090 GPU.

FIGURE 7. Average relative differences of Accuracy & Cost between FZ Setting and the 5 other Training and Evaluation Settings. FZ Setting as Baseline.

serting it is negligible. Paired over the 153 dataset–backbone
cells at 2242 , dropping the crops lowers accuracy in 133
of them, by a mean of 0.64 points and a median of 0.36
(Wilcoxon p = 7 × 10−17 ); the change stays within one point
in 71% of cells, and the largest losses are 6.94 (Cotton, BEiT
B-16) and 6.56 (SoyGene, ResNet-101). For CALMix the
corresponding loss is 0.43 points over 64 cells (p = 2×10−7 ).
The reduction is therefore small and systematic rather than
absent: it buys back the throughput of a single forward pass
at a consistent but bounded accuracy price, which is the tradeoff we recommend, not a free one.
The 448 side sweep isolates CALMix’s own contribution,
because there each backbone was trained both ways on the
same datasets—four CNNs on all three, and ViT B-16 on
CUB alone: adding cross-image mixing to CAL gains 0.34
points with inference crops and 0.46 without, a 4.5% and
6.1% reduction in relative error over the 13 backbone–dataset
pairs (Table 7). The gain is consistent in sign but small, which
is why we present CALMix as a refinement of data-aware
training rather than as the paper’s main result.
VOLUME 11, 2023

Overall, while FGIR-specific training strategies consistently improve accuracy, they also introduce substantial computational overhead. CAL-NC and CALMix-NC mitigate the
inference inefficiency of their original counterparts while
maintaining comparable accuracy. As a result, CAL-NC and
CALMix-NC provide a strong accuracy–efficiency tradeoff, making them practical choices for real-world FGIR applications.
For context against specialized FGIR methods, published
results reach 93.1 on CUB (HERBS [13]) and 94.7 on FGVCAircraft (CMAL-Net [12]), typically at higher input resolutions; CAL itself reports 94.2 on Aircraft with inferencetime cropping [4]. Our best no-crop configurations reach 91.5
(CUB) and 92.8 (Aircraft) at 2242 (Table 3), and 92.4 (CUB,
ConvNeXt-L) and 94.8 (Aircraft, ConvNeXt-B) at 3842 (Table 6)—on Aircraft comparable to both published crop-based
results, and on CUB within 0.7 of HERBS, without the second
forward pass.

7

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

F. SENSITIVITY TO THE LEARNING RATE

The learning-rate search that precedes the main sweep also
measures how fragile each setting is: for every dataset–
backbone–setting cell we take the coefficient of variation
of validation accuracy across the learning rates tried, so a
large value marks a configuration that only works when the
learning rate is right. Because the frozen setting was swept
over a different range, we compare on the 4 learning rates the
fine-tuned settings share, and on the 3 that also include FZ.
The data-aware training recipe makes training substantially less sensitive to the learning rate: over the 15 datasets
searched (all but the two largest, DAFB and iNaturalist), the
mean coefficient of variation falls from 28.0 (FT) to 18.6
(CAL), with CAL the more stable of the two in 122 of 163
dataset–backbone cells (Wilcoxon p = 4 × 10−12 ), and to
14.2 for CALMix against CAL’s 19.2 on the 3 datasets where
both were searched. FZ is not part of this ordering—on the
grid it shares with the others it is indistinguishable from FT
(p = 0.27)—so the effect follows from the CAL recipe rather
than from optimizing fewer parameters. Which component of
that recipe—the attention-guided augmentation, the counterfactual objective, or bilinear attention pooling—is responsible
cannot be separated by the settings evaluated here.
Fragility tracks the same axes as accuracy. Across the 9
core backbones it is strongly anti-correlated with accuracy
(ρ = −0.92, p = 0.0005), ranging from 11.4 for BiT-M
ResNet-101x3 to 44.5 for ResNet-101; ImageNet-21k pretraining lowers it for both backbones available under both
pretraining schemes (Swin-B 21.6 against 27.6, lower in 8 of
8 cells, p = 0.008; ConvNeXt-B 23.5 against 38.0, lower
in 7 of 8, p = 0.055, with SoyLocal under CAL moving
the other way); and across datasets it grows as RIC falls
(ρ = −0.78, p = 0.0006). The accuracy spread a change
of learning rate induces is roughly 5 times the spread across
seeds (FT: 6.9 against 1.3 points, Table 5). The backbones
and datasets this study finds hardest are also those whose
results depend most on tuning, which adds a robustness
argument to the accuracy and cost case for the data-aware
settings.
G. A REPRESENTATIONAL VIEW: INTER-STAGE CKA

The analyses above characterize what accuracy and cost result from each design choice, but not why some backbones,
datasets, and settings succeed while others fail. To address
this, we analyze the internal representations of the trained
models using linear centered kernel alignment (CKA). We
study the 9 core backbones under the FZ, FT, and CAL
settings—all 17 datasets at 2242 and the 4 representative
datasets at 3842 , so 9 × 17 × 3 + 9 × 4 × 3 = 567 runs
from the serial-21 checkpoints. The no-crop variants reuse the
CAL-trained checkpoints and differ only at inference, so their
training-split representations coincide with CAL’s; CALMix
checkpoints exist only for the 4 representative datasets and
are omitted. For each run, we extract unpooled features at
the output of the four network stages—the stage outputs of
hierarchical models (BiT-M ResNet-101x3 uses the same four
8

residual stages as the other ResNets, and VGG-19 the outputs
of its four deepest convolutional blocks), or four equally
spaced blocks for isotropic Transformers (blocks 3, 6, 9, and
12 of 12)—over the entire training split at batch size 8, and
compute the linear CKA between every pair of stages. We
summarize each run by the average CKA of the first stage
against the deeper stages (stage-1 CKA); a low value means
the network produces strongly differentiated representations
across its depth, whereas a high value means the stages are
largely redundant.
Inter-stage feature differentiation predicts accuracy. As
shown in Fig. 8, stage-1 CKA is strongly negatively correlated
with Top-1 accuracy: pooled over all 567 runs the correlation
is Pearson r = −0.75 (Spearman ρ = −0.69, p < 10−80 ),
and it is in fact slightly tighter within each individual setting
(r = −0.76 to −0.79), confirming it is an intra-setting effect
rather than an artifact of comparing settings. Reported pvalues are uncorrected; the main effect (p < 10−80 ) remains
significant under Bonferroni or Benjamini–Hochberg correction across the full test battery, while individual per-setting
and per-metric cells are treated as descriptive. Models that
transform their representation across depth—building progressively more specialized features—achieve higher finegrained accuracy, whereas models whose stages remain mutually redundant perform poorly, independently of parameter
count or computational cost.
The predictive signal is concentrated in early stages.
Comparing depth summaries (Fig. 9), the stage-1-vs-deeper
summary is the strongest predictor in every setting (ρ up
to −0.73), the mean inter-stage summary is intermediate
(ρ = −0.63 pooled), and the last-stage-vs-rest summary
is the weakest (ρ = −0.53). Resolving the CKA matrices
into individual stage pairs confirms the same ordering—
the earliest pairs carry the signal while the deepest pair is
uninformative—so the simple stage-1 summary is the recommended single-number descriptor. This indicates that finegrained recognition depends on how strongly a backbone reshapes its early-stage features, contrasting with the common
transfer-learning view that early layers are generic and only
late layers need adaptation.
A representational view of dataset difficulty. The RIC
grouping introduced in Section IV-A orders datasets by difficulty; at the dataset level, inter-stage CKA separates the two
ends of that ordering, distinguishing the ultra-fine-grained
leaf datasets from the natural-image ones. CKA decreases
with the RIC order of magnitude (Fig. 10; Spearman ρ =
−0.50, p = 0.043) and increases as accuracy falls (Fig. 11;
ρ = −0.52, p = 0.033); these dataset-level correlations
are modest and driven by the two clusters visible in the
figures, in contrast to the run-level effect above. The ultrafine-grained leaf datasets (SoyLocal, SoyGlobal, SoyGene,
SoyAgeing, Cotton) sit at high inter-stage CKA (∼ 0.7):
on these domains the ImageNet-pretrained backbones build
little differentiated feature hierarchy, the stages remain nearredundant (representational collapse), and accuracy is low.
Natural-image datasets (Dogs, Pets, Food, Flowers, CUB,
VOLUME 11, 2023

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

Cars) differentiate strongly (CKA ∼ 0.35–0.45) and score
highly. Thus RIC and inter-stage CKA are complementary
descriptors of difficulty—one based on data statistics, the
other on learned representations—and together they suggest
that the hardest FGIR regimes are limited by representation
VOLUME 11, 2023

Per-class difficulty and follow-up directions. Finally, a
per-class analysis is consistent with this picture (Fig. 12). On
CUB only 3.5% of classes (7 of 200) are hard for most backbones, and on FGVC-Aircraft only 2%—difficulty is concentrated in a small set of intrinsically ambiguous classes. On
the ultra-fine-grained datasets, however, 84% (SoyGene) and
91% (SoyLocal) of classes fall below 50% mean accuracy,
and difficulty is largely backbone-independent. This suggests
that for ultra-fine-grained recognition, domain-adapted pretraining and representation-level interventions are likely to be
more effective than backbone substitution, which we identify
as a promising direction for future work. More broadly, the
CKA–accuracy relationships reported here are correlational;
a controlled depth-freezing or stage-wise linear-probing experiment is needed to establish causality, and we leave this
intervention to future work.

9

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

image ones. Overall, these results highlight the effectiveness
of FGIR-specific, data-aware training and demonstrate that
carefully designed efficient variants can achieve a strong
balance between accuracy and computational cost, providing
practical guidance for future cost-aware FGIR system design.

V. CONCLUSION

Overall, our results show that stronger performance is generally accompanied by higher computational cost. Across
backbones, we observe a non-uniform accuracy–efficiency
relationship, with modern architectures such as Swin and
ConvNeXt achieving the best trade-offs. Similarly, increasing input resolution generally improves accuracy but incurs
higher computational cost, and under the frozen setting the
mean gain is negative over the 9 core backbones, driven by
a collapse of BEiT B-16 at 3842 ; naive resolution scaling is
therefore not an efficient strategy. For training and evaluation
settings, more complex configurations consistently improve
accuracy by up to 78% in relative terms (CALMix), while
increasing training time to roughly 17× the frozen baseline
and reducing inference throughput by up to 86%. In particular, CAL improves accuracy significantly over FT but
introduces higher computational cost in both training and
inference. Building on this, CALMix further enhances performance, while evaluating the same trained models without
inference-time cropping (CAL-NC and CALMix-NC) costs
0.64 points on average across 153 dataset–backbone cells
while restoring near-frozen throughput, indicating that most
of the gain comes from the training recipe rather than from
two-pass inference. These settings also differ in how much
tuning they demand: data-aware augmentation reduces the
accuracy spread across the learning-rate grid by about a
third relative to plain fine-tuning, and the backbones and
datasets that score lowest are also those whose results depend most on that choice. Beyond the accuracy–cost tradeoff, our representational analysis shows that fine-grained accuracy is tightly linked to how strongly a backbone differentiates its features across stages: inter-stage CKA predicts
accuracy (ρ = −0.69 for the early-stage summary) and,
at the dataset level, separates the hardest ultra-fine-grained
datasets—which exhibit representational collapse that backbone substitution alone does not resolve—from the natural10

This section provides visual examples complementing the
CALMix description in the main text. Fig. 13 illustrates
the three data augmentation operations applied for CALMix
training across 3 representative datasets: FGVC-Aircraft [21],
Stanford Cars [24], and CUB-200-2011 [22].
Each figure shows four rows: the original sample images
(Samples), cropping (Crop), masking (Mask), and mixup
(Mix). The Crop and Mask operations are inherited from CAL
[4], while the Mix operation is the additional augmentation
introduced by CALMix, which swaps discriminative regions
between images of the same class to encourage region-level
feature learning.
Across all three domains, the Mix operation exposes the
model to a broader distribution of discriminative region combinations within the same class. The measured effect is a
small mean gain rather than a uniform one: CALMix is below
CAL in 4 of the 13 backbone–dataset pairs with inference
crops (Table 7), and the mean gain is smaller than the seed-toseed standard deviation of CALMix itself (Table 5). However,
this richer augmentation pipeline increases the number of
training samples processed per epoch, which accounts for the
longer training times observed for CALMix relative to CAL
in the settings analysis.
APPENDIX B BACKBONE AND SETTING SELECTION
GUIDE

To assist practitioners in applying our findings, Fig. 14 provides a decision flowchart for how to select a backbone and
training & evaluation settings given a new FGIR dataset. The
selection process first considers the primary computational
constraint: (1) maximizing accuracy, (2) minimizing training
cost, or (3) minimizing inference cost. It then considers the
dataset difficulty, characterized by the order of magnitude
(OOM) of the Ratio of Images to Classes (RIC) introduced
in the main text.
The backbone recommendations within each branch follow
the RIC analysis: Swin-B [39] leads in the most challenging
regimes (RIC orders of magnitude −3 and −2), by roughly 11
points on the single group −3 dataset and 3 points averaged
over the three group −2 datasets, while ConvNeXt-B [40]
leads in the moderate regime (RIC −1). In the easiest regimes
(0 and 1) the top backbones lie within half a point of each
other, so backbone choice matters most as dataset difficulty
increases; this ordering is also reflected in the CKA analysis,
where Swin-B’s hierarchical window attention differentiates
its representations more strongly on the hardest datasets. The
setting recommendations follow from the settings analysis:
when training cost is constrained, a frozen setup avoids the
roughly 17× training-time factor of the most complex setting.

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

computational constraints and dataset difficulty. Difficulty is the order of magnitude of RIC = Ntrain /C 2 as defined in the main text, with Ntrain the number
of training images and C the number of classes; the abbreviated note inside the figure is superseded by that definition.

Finally, when compute for training is available but inference cost is constrained, CAL-NC and CALMix-NC recover
throughput to near-FZ level while keeping most of the accuracy benefit of data-aware training.

means include these runs. The no-crop settings reuse their
parents’ checkpoints rather than retraining: the accuracy files
record no per-seed spread for CAL-NC, while CALMixNC’s spread reflects the underlying CALMix training seeds
evaluated without crops.

APPENDIX C TRAINING PROTOCOL

Across the 4 trained settings of the main multi-seed
sweep—the no-crop variants re-evaluate these checkpoints at
inference and add no training runs—the experiment log totals
2,229 runs: 525 each for FZ, FT, and CAL at image size
224; 128 each for FZ, FT, and CAL at image size 384 (16
backbones, 4 datasets, 2 seeds); 192 for CALMix at image
size 224; and 39 each for CAL and CALMix at image size
448. At image size 224 the two largest datasets (DAFB and
iNaturalist) use 2 seeds instead of 3, which is why those
settings total 525 rather than 543 runs each. CALMix was
additionally trained and timed at image size 384 on CUB for
the cost measurements (a 17× to 21× training-time factor
over the frozen baseline, depending on resolution), but it was
never evaluated for accuracy at that resolution and has no
inference-cost measurement there, so it is excluded from both

Table 4 summarizes the training hyperparameters shared
across runs. Values follow the defaults of the training codebase unless a setting explicitly overrides them, as noted in the
table.
APPENDIX D SEED VARIANCE AND EXPERIMENT COUNT

Table 5 reports, per setting, the standard deviation of accuracy
across the training seeds (Section C), aggregated over all
dataset–backbone pairs. Fine-tuning (FT) is the most seedsensitive setting by mean, and its max is more than twice
that of any other setting; the frozen-backbone setting (FZ) is
the least seed-sensitive at both image sizes. The FT maxima
trace to isolated training collapses of BEiT B-16 (std 44.45
on NABirds and 25.10 on Flowers, over 3 seeds); reported

APPENDIX E RESULTS AT IMAGE SIZE 384

Table 6 reports every backbone at 3842 on the 4 representative
datasets, under CAL and under its no-crop evaluation. It is
the source of the two 3842 figures quoted in the settings
analysis: the best no-crop results are 94.84 on FGVC-Aircraft
(ConvNeXt-B, IN21k) and 92.41 on CUB (ConvNeXt-L,
IN21k). Removing the inference crops costs nothing at this
resolution — the best no-crop result is at least as high as the
best crop-based one on FGVC-Aircraft, CUB and SoyLocal,
and lower only on SoyGene — which is the same conclusion
the 2242 settings analysis reaches, at a resolution where the
absolute numbers are competitive with published crop-based

VOLUME 11, 2023

=== PAPER: Cultivar Identification from Leaf Images with a Semantic Adaptation Warmup for Frozen Vision Transformers ===

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

1

Cultivar Identification from Leaf Images with a
Semantic Adaptation Warmup for Frozen Vision Transformers
Edwin Arkel Rios Student Member, IEEE, Femiloye Oyerinde, Fernando Mikael, Oswin Gosal, Min-Chun
Hu, Senior Member, IEEE, and Bo-Cheng Lai

Abstract—Identifying the cultivar of a crop from its leaves
underpins breeding programs and seed certification, but it is
done by hand: the visual differences between cultivars are
finer than those between species, and labeled data is scarce.
The usual remedy, adapting a network pretrained on a generic
corpus, transfers poorly: across thirteen configurations built on
a frozen Vision Transformer, accuracy falls from between 82.9%
and 88.7% on a species-level benchmark to at most 69.7% on
the easiest of ten cultivar datasets, and a linear classifier on
the frozen representation reaches only 17.9% on SoyGlobal.
We attribute this to a semantic gap between what generic
pretraining encodes and what separating cultivars requires, and
we close it with a Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW): the
inserted adaptation modules are first trained against a supervised
contrastive objective on a pooled corpus of cultivar imagery, and
only then is the classifier trained. The warmup is driven by
a Dual-Attention-driven Mix Augmentation SupCon (DAMAS)
strategy, which locates discriminative regions from the attention
of two early transformer layers rather than a full rollout,
reducing the augmentation cost from the O(L · N 2 ) of a [CLS]row rollout to O(N 2 ). Warmup happens once and adds nothing
to inference cost. Across ten cultivar datasets at 448 × 448 the
warmup raises average top-1 accuracy by 5.9 points over the
same architecture without it, exceeds the strongest competing
parameter-efficient method by 12.6 points and the strongest fully
fine-tuned method in our comparison by 5.1 points, while training
0.51 M parameters per 200-class task, at lower FLOPs and higher
throughput than the alternatives.
Index Terms—Cultivar identification, plant phenotyping, precision agriculture, ultra-fine-grained image recognition, parameterefficient transfer learning, vision transformer, contrastive learning

I. I NTRODUCTION
This article is an extended version of work presented at the Efficient Deep
Learning for Foundation Models workshop at ECCV 2024 [1]. The inter-layer
adapter and its evaluation appeared there; the semantic adaptation warmup, the
gap measurement that motivates it, and all warmup experiments and ablations
are new.

Taiwan, under grants NSTC 111-2221-E-A49-092-MY3, NSTC 113-2640-EA49-005, NSTC 112-2221-E-007-079-MY3 and NSTC 113-2218-E-007-020.

and NYCU HPC for providing computational and storage resources.

T

ELLING one cultivar of a crop from another by eye is
a routine and consequential task in agriculture. Breeding
programmes must track which variety is growing in which
plot, and seed certification depends on confirming varietal
identity; both are done today by trained inspectors working
from leaf morphology. It is slow, and it does not scale: expert
accuracy falls as the number of candidate cultivars grows, and
annotating images to train a model requires the same scarce
expertise, so labeled data stays thin — often only a handful
of images per cultivar. Figure 1 shows what makes the task
hard: leaves of different cultivars look nearly identical, while
leaves of one cultivar vary widely.

Fig. 1. Samples of different classes from the ultra-fine-grained SoyGene
and SoyLocal datasets [2], highlighting subtle inter-class differences and
pronounced intra-class diversity.

Automated visual recognition in agrifood has advanced
quickly on tasks of this shape. Deep networks now classify
potato breeds from tuber and leaf imagery [3], grade defects
in soybean kernels [4], identify individual cattle from muzzle
patterns [5], detect plant disease in real time from a drone [6],
and discriminate visually similar seedlings under varying field
conditions [7]. What these share with cultivar identification is
that the distinction to be made is finer than the species level,
so the discriminative evidence is subtle, distributed, and easily
swamped by variation within a class. This regime is known
as ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) [2], and it
separates cultivar identification from conventional fine-grained
recognition, which stops at species-level granularity [8], [9].

A. The problem: pretrained features do not transfer to cultivar
granularity
The standard recipe for a task with few labels is to take
a network pretrained on a large generic corpus and adapt it.
For cultivar recognition that recipe fails at the first step, and
it fails by a wide margin.
We measured this directly. Holding a Vision Transformer’s
[10] pretrained weights frozen and training only lightweight
modules on top, we evaluated thirteen such configurations —
those of Tab. II that were run on both benchmarks — at

0000–0000/00$00.00 © 2021 IEEE

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

224×224 resolution on a species-level fine-grained benchmark
(CUB-200-2011, birds) and ten cultivar-level leaf datasets. On
the species benchmark every configuration reaches between
82.9% and 88.7% top-1 accuracy. On the cultivar datasets the
best of those same thirteen configurations, on the easiest of
the ten datasets, reaches 69.7%, and the weakest combinations
fall below 10%. Every configuration on the species benchmark
outperforms every configuration on every cultivar dataset, with
13.2 points separating the worst of the former from the best of
the latter. A plain linear classifier on the frozen representation
illustrates the same point at its starkest: 17.9% on SoyGlobal
and 19.5% on SoyGene.
The shortfall is visible in the representation itself, before
any adaptation is attempted. We computed the layer-wise
Centered Kernel Alignment [11] of the pretrained encoder on
all eleven datasets under one protocol, with no training of
any kind. Averaged over the deepest third of the encoder,
similarity between layers is 0.56 to 0.57 on the species
benchmark but between 0.709 and 0.743 on every one of the
ten cultivar datasets, at both input resolutions we evaluate: the
two ranges never overlap, on any dataset, at either resolution.
On cultivar imagery the deep layers of the pretrained encoder
have largely stopped distinguishing one layer’s output from
another’s, which is the representational form of the same
failure the accuracies report. A second, independent measure
agrees. At matched class count, the log maximum evidence
[12] of the frozen features is 1.50 on the species benchmark
against 1.23 to 1.26 on every cultivar dataset of comparable
granularity.
This is not a shortage of data. SoyGene carries more training
images than the species benchmark and still scores far lower.
It is a mismatch between what generic pretraining encodes and
what separating cultivars requires — a semantic gap between
the pretraining domain and the target task. Closing it, rather
than merely adding capacity on top of it, is what this article
addresses.
The gap also explains why parameter-efficient adaptation
is the natural setting here rather than an afterthought. Fully
fine-tuning a transformer for every cultivar task is expensive to store and to serve, and deployment in this domain
means a handheld or airborne device rather than a datacenter.
Parameter-efficient transfer learning (PETL) methods [13]–
[15] answer that by freezing the backbone and training small
inserted modules, so one copy of the backbone serves many
tasks. But a frozen backbone is exactly the case where the gap
bites hardest, because the representation cannot move to meet
the task. Two symptoms follow. The deeper encoder layers
converge toward nearly identical representations, a degradation
of representational diversity known as attention collapse [16]–
[19] and visible in the layer-wise similarity of Fig. 4. And
the attention that remains settles on background rather than
on the leaf, as Fig. 2 shows — a shortcut [20] that a model
with few labeled images is readily driven to. A method that
adapts a frozen backbone for cultivar recognition therefore has
to address the gap explicitly.

2

Fig. 2. Samples and attention maps on SoyAgeing [2] for different PETL
methods: linear classifier ViT [10], Adapter [21], ILA (Ours) and SAW (Ours).
Previous baselines display higher attention to background regions.

B. Approach
We introduce a Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW): a
stage that trains the inserted adaptation modules against a
supervised contrastive objective, on a pooled corpus of cultivar
imagery, before the classifier is trained. Its augmentation is
data-aware — discriminative regions are located from the
attention of two early transformer layers and swapped between
images, which costs O(N 2 ) rather than the O(L · N 2 ) of
a full attention rollout [22]–[24], where L is the number
of transformer layers and N the sequence length. We call
this augmentation Dual-Attention-driven Mix Augmentation
SupCon (DAMAS). Earlier work has added self-supervised or
contrastive losses to fine-tuning [25]–[28], but trains them on
the target dataset alone and augments without regard to where
the discriminative evidence lies. The warmup adds nothing
to inference cost: it changes only the state the modules start
from. Nor is it tied to the module it initializes: applied to a
plain adapter, with no inter-layer module at all, it is worth 8.8
points across the ten datasets.
Across ten cultivar datasets, the warmup raises average
top-1 accuracy by 5.9 points at 448 × 448 over the same
architecture trained without it, and by 6.7 points at 224 × 224;
against the strongest competing parameter-efficient method,
FFVT, the margins are 12.6 and 11.1 points respectively. It
trains 0.51 M parameters per task, of which the 200-class head
is 0.15 M — the head scales with the label set. Inference costs
67.1 GFLOPs against 78.5 for the frozen baseline, with higher
batched throughput than every parameter-efficient method we
compare against.

C. Relation to our earlier work
This article extends a paper published at the Efficient Deep
Learning for Foundation Models workshop at ECCV 2024 [1].
That paper contributed:
1) an Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) that spatially downsamples the token grid between frozen encoder blocks,
introducing a hierarchical inductive bias that counters
attention collapse while reducing inference cost; and

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

2) an evaluation across ten cultivar datasets against more
than fifteen methods, establishing that the adapter is
competitive with fully fine-tuned models while training
a small fraction of the parameters — 123× fewer
deployable parameters, summed across the ten datasets,
than the fully fine-tuned methods compared.
The adapter and its design ablation are therefore prior work,
summarized here as the architecture this article builds upon.
New to this article are:
1) the semantic adaptation warmup and its DAMAS augmentation, with the efficient two-layer attention formulation described above;
2) the measurement of the pretraining-to-cultivar gap reported above, which motivates the warmup and quantifies the problem the earlier paper left implicit;
3) ablations isolating the warmup’s components — its
augmentation, its objective, and the breadth of the corpus
it is trained on;
4) evidence that the warmup is independent of the adapter
it initializes: applied to a plain adapter with no interlayer module anywhere, it raises average accuracy by
8.8 points and helps on all ten datasets;
5) a comparison across pretraining strategies, showing the
warmup transfers between backbones; and
6) results at a second input resolution across all ten
datasets, together with insertion-location and downsampling-rate studies.

3

On the other side, self-supervised learning has also become
crucial to learn discriminative features with limited data [25]–
[27]. Foundational contrastive methods like SimCLR [34] and
SupCon [35] define positive pairs via data augmentations or
class labels, but their use of generic augmentations limits
performance in fine-grained tasks. Recent extensions, such as
PID [36] and SelfCon [37], introduce stronger cross-image
augmentations [38] or auxiliary modules that increase training
complexity or overlook subtle details critical for UFGIR.
Therefore, current SOTA methods such as MixViT [25],
CLE-ViT [26] and CSDNet [27] have incorporated selfsupervised learning to further enhance the extraction of ultrafine discriminative features; most recently, GAEor [39] sets
the fully fine-tuned state of the art on these benchmarks by
encoding geometric attributes of amplified details as a selfsupervised signal on a Swin-B backbone. However, they employ generic or weak data augmentation which limits feature
learning with scarce data. Additionally, most of these methods
employ transformer-based backbones with a large number of
parameters that all need to be stored for deployment and
require significant resources during training.
To address these limitations our method uses ViT attention
to identify and swap discriminative regions in image pairs
for label-aware contrastive learning. This approach enhances
representation learning under limited data constraints through
the usage of cross-image data-aware augmented positives.
Furthermore, we employ parameter-efficient transfer learning
to reduce number of deployable parameters and training costs,
ensuring our method remains scalable and deployment-friendly
while preserving ultra-fine-grained recognition performance.

II. R ELATED W ORK
A. Ultra Fine-Grained Image Recognition

B. Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning

Ultra fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) remains a
challenging task due to subtle inter-class differences and
limited labeled data. To address this, many methods build
on generic recognition backbones and augment them with
modules or loss functions that effectively select and aggregate discriminative features [29]–[32]. For instance, FFVT
[30] leverages ViT attention scores to extract and aggregate
intermediate low-, medium-, and high-level features through
the last transformer encoder block, enhancing sensitivity to
subtle fine-grained differences.

Parameter-efficient transfer learning (PETL) techniques seek
to fine-tune only a small subset of a model’s parameters while
keeping the majority of the pretrained backbone fixed. These
methods generally fall into two categories: prompt-tuning and
adapter-based approaches.

Given the data constraints in UFGIR, tailored data augmentation strategies [2], [31]–[33] are critical. Earlier methods such as SPARE [32] employ CNNs to extract semantic
regions for augmentation. More recent methods make use
of transformer aggregated attention for data-aware augmentations [23], [24], but incur high computational costs since
they employ attention rollout for aggregation [22] which has
complexity of O(L · N 3 ) with respect to the number of
transformer layers L and the sequence length N . In contrast,
our method uses an efficient implementation based on two
early ViT layers, drastically reducing complexity to O(N 2 ).

Prompt-tuning [13] augments the input sequence with additional task-specific, learnable tokens appended at various
stages of the transformer. For instance, VQT [40] employs
these tokens as queries to aggregate layer-wise information,
which is then integrated into the classification head. However,
the incorporation of extra tokens not only increases the computational cost during the forward pass but also leads to a
rapid increase in the parameters for the classification head.
In contrast, adapter-based methods insert lightweight, nonlinear modules directly within transformer layers. Originally
proposed by Houlsby et al. [21] in the natural language
processing domain, adapters have been successfully adapted
for vision transformers. For example, ConvPass [41] and ProTuning [14] extend this concept by integrating 2D convolutions to inject spatial biases, thereby enhancing the extraction

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

4

downsampling; this design enables residual gradient flow critical for incorporating downsampling in parameter-efficient setting. During the SAW stage, dualattention rollout from early layers guides data-aware augmentation through our Dual-Attention Mix Augmented SupCon (DAMAS) strategy, pretraining only
the adapter modules using a supervised contrastive loss. After warmup, the projection head is discarded and replaced by a classification head for fine-tuning
on the target task.

of fine-grained features. However, these existing PETL methods do not incorporate hierarchical biases, such as features
downsampling, which impacts the performance of UFGIR
tasks under data scarcity.

a classification head and train the model to predict the class
label based on a cross-entropy loss.

A. Vision Transformer Encoder
III. M ETHOD
The overview of our method is shown in Fig. 3. Our InterLayer Adapter (ILA) mitigates attention collapse [16]–[19] by
enforcing spatial hierarchical feature extraction via lightweight
adapters inserted between frozen ViT layers. The proposed
ILA employs two branches, Spatial Downsampling (SDS)
branch and Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS), to enable
residual gradient flow necessary for spatial downsampling in
the parameter-efficient setting.
Furthermore, to support better initialization for the adapter
modules we propose employing a Semantic Adaptation
Warmup (SAW) stage with Dual-Attention Mix Augmented
SupCon (DAMAS) strategy. During the SAW stage, dataaware cross-image mix augmentations are produced through
the use of an efficient dual-layer attention rollout mechanism.
Extracted features from the original images and augmented
are passed through a projection head to obtain representations
for a supervised contrastive loss. This loss enforces the model
to learn discriminative details to push apart representations
from different images and classes (negative pairs) and pull
closer images corresponding to the same class (positive pairs).
In this stage only the parameter-efficient modules are trained
while the encoder parameters are kept frozen. Subsequently,
in the finetuning stage we replace the projection head with

The Vision Transformer (ViT) encoder processes an input
image X ∈ RIS×IS×C , where IS denotes the image side
length and C the number of channels. The image is patchified
using a convolution with kernel size and stride P × P ,
producing SZ1 = IS/P patches along each spatial dimension.
These patches are flattened and linearly projected into Ddimensional embeddings, forming an input token sequence of
length N1 = SZ21 . A learnable classification token ([CLS])
is prepended to the sequence, and positional embeddings are
added to preserve spatial information. The resulting sequence
is then passed through a series of L transformer encoder
blocks, each consisting of multi-head self-attention (MHSA)
and position-wise feed-forward networks (PWFFN) [42]; the
output of each encoder block is denoted as Zl . Finally, a
LayerNorm [43] followed by a linear classifier is applied to
the [CLS] token for prediction.

B. Attention Collapse in Vision Transformers
Recent studies have identified a phenomenon known as
attention collapse in Vision Transformers (ViTs) [17], [19],
where attention weights between tokens become increasingly
uniform across layers. This over-smoothing [44] leads to loss
of high-frequency information and a reduction in representational diversity. Such uniformity is particularly detrimental in

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR), where nuanced
inter-class variations must be preserved and emphasized.
The problem is further exacerbated in parameter-efficient
transfer learning (PETL) settings, where only a small number
of additional parameters (e.g., adapters) are fine-tuned while
the pretrained ViT backbone remains frozen. In such settings,
we observe that the attention matrices of deeper transformer
layers become extremely similar, as measured by the Centered
Kernel Alignment (CKA) similarity shown in Fig. 4. This
indicates redundancy in the feature encoding process, limiting
the ability of the model to extract discriminative features
necessary for fine-grained tasks and highlighting the need
for specialized mechanisms that preserve or enhance attention
diversity during adaptation.

5

C. ILA: Inter-Layer Adapter
To mitigate attention collapse and encourage the model
to attend to distinct spatial regions throughout the encoding
process, we introduce the Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) module.
ILA explicitly imposes a hierarchical structure by progressively reducing the spatial resolution of token embeddings
between groups of transformer encoder blocks. This design
draws inspiration from prior work that embeds hierarchical
inductive biases into the model architecture for richer feature
encoding [45], [46].
We divide the transformer encoder into three groups, each
operating at a different spatial resolution. The spatial resolution at group i is denoted by SZi × SZi , where i ∈ {1, 2, 3}
and SZ1 > SZ2 > SZ3 . Transitions between these groups are
implemented using the ILA module, which performs spatial
downsampling according to:

SZi+1 = SZi − (KS − 1),

(1)

where KS is the kernel size of the depthwise convolution.
The token sequence zl ∈ RNi ×D at stage i (with Ni = SZ2i )
is first reshaped into a 2D feature map of shape SZi ×SZi ×D
to facilitate convolutional processing.

Fig. 4. Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) similarity [11] between attention
layers of a ViT after undergoing parameter-efficient fine-tuning on the Cotton
[2] dataset. Top-left: baseline without PETL modules. Top-right: ViT with
standard Adapters [21]. Bottom-left: ViT with the proposed Inter-Layer
Adapter (ILA). Bottom-right: ViT with ILA and the proposed SAW twostage training strategy. Lighter colors indicate higher inter-layer similarity,
reflecting increased redundancy. The top-left, top-right and bottom-left panels
are reproduced from our earlier workshop paper [1].

The ILA consists of two branches: the Spatial Downsampling (SDS) branch and the Residual Spatial Downsampling
(RSDS) branch. While the structure of the SDS resembles
the convolutional branch proposed in [41], our design introduces two key differences: (1) we use depthwise separable
convolution [47] to reduce computation, and (2) we omit
padding to explicitly reduce spatial resolution at each stage.
Furthermore, the RSDS branch provides a novel residual path
to preserve information across groups. These design choices
encourage feature diversity by enforcing hierarchical encoding
while reducing computational costs.
1) Spatial Downsampling Branch (SDS): The SDS branch
takes as input the token sequence Zl ∈ R(1+Ni )×D at group
i, where Ni = SZ2i , and performs spatial downsampling
through three main steps: (1) channel downsampling (CDS),
(2) depthwise separable convolution (DWSConv), and (3)
channel upsampling (CUS).
First, a projection reduces the channel dimension from D to
a smaller dimension d (d ≪ D), producing zCDS
∈ RNi ×d :

This design allows the RSDS branch to behave as an identity
function at the beginning of training when KS = 1, with
the capacity to adapt its weights to modulate the influence of
the original features, effectively acting as a learnable gating
mechanism [49] as training progresses. At the kernel sizes
actually deployed (KS > 1) the convolution at initialization
behaves as a sum-pooling operation instead, and it remains
trainable. Inspired by the transition from hand-crafted filters
to learnable ones in early convolutional networks such as
AlexNet [50], we adopt this strategy to introduce learnable
residual downsampling. This design provides richer inductive
bias than static methods while still retaining stability via nearidentity initialization.

2) Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS) branch: To
preserve local information and stabilize training by ensuring
gradient flow between encoder groups [48], we integrate a
learnable residual connection via the Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS) branch. This parallel path compensates for
the reduction in spatial resolution introduced by the SDS
branch. Unlike fixed pooling or interpolation which can erase
fine-grained spatial cues, RSDS uses a 2D depthwise convolution applied to the patch tokens only, leaving the [CLS] token
unchanged to preserve its global information.
Let zl ∈ R(1+Ni )×D denote the input token sequence,
where the first token corresponds to [CLS]. After removing
the [CLS] token and reshaping the remaining patch tokens
into a spatial feature map Zl ∈ RSZi ×SZi ×D , we apply a
depthwise convolution with the same kernel size KS as in
the SDS branch to each channel c ∈ {0, 1, . . . , D − 1}:

which is passed to the next encoder group. This additive
fusion allows the model to retain both the refined spatial
features from SDS and the residual identity-preserving path
from RSDS.

D. Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) with DAMAS
To further address the challenges brought by data scarcity
in UFGIR, observed in Fig. 2 as overfitting to background
regions, we propose a Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW)
stage that allows our ViT with ILA modules to bridge the
semantic gap [28] between generic pretraining and ultra-finegrained recognition.
To address this issue we propose a novel strategy composed
of the three following components:
1) Data Diversity: we pool all ultra-fine-grained datasets to
create a rich, diverse corpus for the adaptation warmup.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

7

Fig. 6. Example of our cross-image data augmentation. Discriminative regions
from original leaf images (left) are swapped between image pairs (right),
producing harder positive samples for ultra-fine-grained recognition.

2) Supervised Contrastive Warmup: we pretrain the
inter-layer adapters using a SupCon loss to align their
features with the frozen ViT backbone.
3) Early Attention Data-Aware Augmentation: Use
dual-attention rollout from two early layers to identify
and swap discriminative regions between image
pairs from the same label, generating diverse, labelaware, cross-image augmentations as positives for the
supervised contrastive loss.
After warmup, adapters are fine-tuned on the target dataset.
As shown in Fig. 4, incorporating this warmup stage results
in deeper transformer layers that capture more distinct and
discriminative features, in contrast to the collapsed representations observed with the frozen backbone alone. Figure 5
shows the same comparison on all ten datasets: averaged over
the deepest third of the encoder, similarity falls from 0.727
for the frozen encoder to 0.661 with adapters and 0.634 with
ILA, and the warmed state is lower still at 0.547.
We use the supervised contrastive loss [35] with a DualAttention-driven Mix Augmentation (DAMAS), since traditional data augmentation techniques apply generic transformations that often fail to capture the subtle, discriminative
details necessary for UFGIR. To generate label-aware positive
samples, we cut and swap discriminative regions, using the
dual-attention rollout, between image pairs from the same class
as seen in Fig. 6. To obtain the embedding r for the SupCon
loss, we first apply global average pooling to the feature ZL
produced by the final transformer block, and then pass the
resulting vector through a MLP projection head.

Fig. 7. Attention maps from aggregated layers (e.g., ”1–4” means layers 1
to 4) on UFGIR leaves datasets, showing the similarity between full attention
rollout (second row) and the proposed dual-attention approach (third and
fourth rows).

among image patches. Attention rollout [22] aggregates attention across layers to highlight regions of focus, and it has
been used for data augmentation [23], [24]. However, full
attention rollout over L layers is computationally expensive —
O(L · N 3 ) with full matrix products, and still O(L · N 2 ) when
only the [CLS] row is propagated — where N is the sequence
length and L is the number of aggregated layers, while relying
on a single layer often produces noisy or inconsistent maps
[51].
To address this, we propose a dual-attention strategy that
combines attention from two early layers, such as layers 2-3
or 3–4, as illustrated in Fig. 7. Specifically, we extract the
first row of the attention matrix (corresponding to the [CLS]
token) from one layer and multiply it with that of the next
layer, reducing the computation to O(N 2 ) — a factor of L
below a [CLS]-row rollout over all layers, and a factor of
L · N below the full-matrix form. The saving matters because
N grows quadratically with image resolution.
The third and fourth rows of Fig. 7 show that our dualattention maps capture discriminative areas comparable to the
full rollout (second row) while being much more efficient.
These discriminative regions guide our data-aware augmentation by swapping regions between image pairs (see Fig. 6),
enhancing fine-grained feature learning and data diversity for
UFGIR tasks.

In Tab. I we describe the datasets used for our experiments.
These are ultra-fine-grained leaves datasets collected by Yu et
al. [2], where each category represents a cultivar attached to
the seed obtained from the genetic resource bank.

over 3,526 cultivar labels, where the SoyAgeing subsets
share their parent’s label space — and contains no test
image of any dataset, verified by matching every image
path in the corpus against every dataset’s test split.

We conduct our experiments in two stages. First, we perform
a learning rate search over {0.003, 0.01, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3} using
a subset of the training data. In the second stage, we train the
model on the full training set using the selected learning rate
and evaluate performance on the test set over three independent runs. Results are reported in terms of top-1 accuracy mean
and standard deviation. When applicable, the best performance
values are highlighted in bold and the second-best values are
underlined.

To verify the effectiveness of the proposed approaches, we
compare against 13 SOTA methods and a linear-probe baseline
in the parameter-efficient setting. These methods are organized
into three Families based on their fine-tuning strategies and
feature aggregation mechanisms. The first category, PE Classifier, focuses on the classification head:

In addition, we assess computational efficiency by measuring 1) task-specific trainable parameters (TSP, the number of
parameters fine-tuned for the task), 2) inference floating-point
operations (FLOPs), and 3) inference throughput in terms of
images processed per second as measured on an NVIDIA RTX
3090. We use PyTorch and WandB [52] to implement and
manage experiments.
All models are optimized using Stochastic Gradient Descent
(SGD) with momentum of 0.9, a batch size of 8, and cosine
learning rate scheduler. We fine-tune models for 50 epochs. If
incorporating the SAW stage, we pretrain the PETL modules
once, for 100 epochs on the pooled corpus, and reuse that
checkpoint for every task; the warmup uses the same optimizer and batch size, a learning rate of 0.1, and a SupCon
temperature τ of 0.07.
For data preprocessing, we resize images to a square of size
300 × 300 or 600 × 600, then crop a random square during
training or a center crop during inference of size 224 × 224
or 448 × 448, respectively. All images are horizontally flipped
and normalized using ImageNet mean and standard deviation.
All experiments employ the ViT B-16 [10] backbone pretrained on ImageNet-21k, with a patch size P of 16, L = 12
transformer layers, and a hidden dimension D of 768. Reduced
adapter dimension d is set to 8. All methods fine-tune the
classification head and the [CLS] token. We propose four
different variations of our method:

ILA: inter-layer adapter modules with downsampling are
inserted by default after layers 4 and 8.
• ILA+: in addition to downsampling ILA modules, nondownsampling adapters with padding are inserted after
all layers except layer 4 and 8.
• ILA++: in addition to downsampling ILA modules, traditional intra-layer adapters [21] are added in every layer.
• SAW: extends ILA++ by integrating a semantic adaptation warmup stage, wherein our proposed Dual-Attentiondriven Mix Augmentation SupCon (DAMAS) strategy
pretrains the adapters on a diverse corpus of ultra-finegrained data before fine-tuning. The pooled corpus is the
union of the ten datasets’ training splits — 24,367 images

•

Linear Classifier (Baseline, ViT B-16): the simplest PE
method, which keeps the backbone frozen and, like every
method here, fine-tunes only the classification head and
the [CLS] token.
• Low-Rank Bilinear Pooling (LR-BLP) [53]: employs a
low-rank projection to reduce the dimensionality of the
bilinearly pooled features.
• Matrix Power Normalized Covariance (MPNC) [54],
[55]: applies covariance pooling of high-level features to
lessen instabilities of bilinear pooling.
• Intermediate Features Aggregation (IFA) [56]: selects
CLS tokens from intermediate layers and forwards them
through a small MLP to aggregate cross-layer features.
•

The next category includes FGIR methods where a module
explicitly select features based on some criteria, along with
a possible module to aggregate these selected discriminative
features. We evaluate these models in the parameter-efficient
setting where only a small percentage of modules are finetuned so we refer to them as PEFGIR. It includes:
CAL [57]: employs counter-factuality to train a bilinear
attention module, which is used for both pooling features
and to generate augmented versions of the input images.
The fine-tuned components include the attention module
and the bilinear attention pooling classification head.
• TransFG [29]: selects features from the previous-to-last
layer based on aggregated attention [22]. We fine-tune
the last transformer encoder block, where the feature
aggregation happens and the linear classification head.
• FFVT [30]: selects and aggregates intermediate features
based on layer-wise attention. The last transformer encoder block and the classification head are fine-tuned.
• RAMS-Trans (RAMS) [23]: crops the image for data
augmentation based on attention rollout [23]. Fine-tunes
only the classification head.
• GLSim [51]: computes the similarity between global and
local representations of an image to select crops. An
aggregator transformer block and the classification head
are fine-tuned.
•

The GRPETL category includes methods proposed for
generic image recognition under PETL settings:
•

VPT-Shallow (VPT-S) [13]: appends learnable prompts
before the transformer encoder.
