VPT-Deep (VPT-D) [13]: appends learnable prompts to
the sequence before each transformer block and then
removes them after each block.
• Visual Query Tuning (VQT) [40]: appends learnable
prompts to be used as queries to the sequence before
the MHSA module of each layer. These prompts are
expedited towards the classification head where they are
concatenated into a single large dimensional linear layer.
• Adapter [21]: incorporates a small MLP inserted after the
MHSA and PWFFN of each transformer encoder block.
• ConvPass (ConvP) [41]: similar to the previous, it incorporates a small MLP inserted inside the transformer
block, but this MLP incorporates a 3 × 3 convolution in
between the channel downsampling and upsampling of
the adapter.

•

We also compare against the SOTA employing ViTs for
UFGIR published by Fang et al. [27]. These methods were
trained under traditional fine-tuning settings, with all of the
backbone parameters being adapted for each task so we refer
to them as FT. It includes:
SIM-Trans (SIM-Tr) [58]: employs structure information
and multi-level contrastive learning.
• CSDNet [27]: incorporates data-aware cropping augmentation, contrastive learning and self-distillation for learning with limited samples.
•

V. R ESULTS AND D ISCUSSION
A. The Pretraining-to-Cultivar Gap
Table V reports the measurement behind the motivation
given in Sec. I, dataset by dataset, on the pretrained encoder
with no training of any kind. Deep-layer CKA separates the

species benchmark from every cultivar dataset at both input
resolutions, and the two ranges do not overlap: 0.569 and 0.561
on CUB-200 against 0.709 to 0.743 across the ten. On cultivar
imagery the deepest third of the encoder has largely stopped
distinguishing one layer’s output from the next.
LogME needs more care, because it grows with the number
of classes. SoyGene and SoyGlobal score above CUB-200 on
it while carrying 1110 and 1938 classes against 200, so a direct
ranking across the column would be misleading. Restricted to
the seven cultivar datasets of comparable granularity, 198 to
200 classes, CUB-200 leads every one of them: 1.499 against
1.235 to 1.256. The same effect is visible in the SoyAgeing
rows, where the full set and its five subsets share 198 classes
and differ fivefold in size yet move LogME by at most 0.014.

B. Accuracy Comparison with State-of-the-Art
1) Evaluation at 224 × 224 Resolution: We evaluate the
effectiveness of our method across five UFGIR datasets at
a resolution of 224 × 224, as summarized in Tab. II. Our
variants provide consistent improvements over the baseline,
yielding gains between 13.5 points (ILA) and 27.4 points
(SAW) in average accuracy across datasets. This highlights
the effectiveness of incorporating hierarchical biases to address
attention collapse.
When combined with conventional adapters [21], the resulting ILA++ variant further improves performance, surpassing
the previous state-of-the-art, FFVT [30], by an average of
5.8 points. These results demonstrate the compatibility of the
proposed module with existing PETL mechanisms.
Incorporating the SAW stage for ILA++ leads to further
accuracy gains, most notably a 12.1-point improvement on the

2) Evaluation at 448 × 448 Resolution: To evaluate the
effectiveness and scalability of our method across image sizes,
we compare results across ten UFGIR datasets employing
448 × 448 image size, shown in Tab. III and Tab. IV. Our
variants consistently improve upon the baseline ViT, increasing

average accuracy across datasets by 15.6 points (ILA) up
to 29.2 points (SAW). This consolidates the advantages of
incorporating hierarchical feature extraction to increase discriminative feature recognition in UFGIR tasks.
The ILA++ variant which also employs intra-layer adapters
[21] surpasses the previous best method in the PETL setting,
FFVT [30], by 6.8 points of average accuracy across tasks.
This further validates the synergy of our hierarchical biases
with existing PETL modules.

After incorporating the SAW stage our model achieves the
best average results across all ten datasets among the compared
methods, surpassing the strongest fully fine-tuned method in
our comparison, CSDNet’s ViT-B variant [27], by 5.1 points.
Fully fine-tuned Swin-B models sit higher still — CSDNet’s
Swin-B variant and the recent GAEor [39] — at one to
two orders of magnitude more task-specific parameters; they
mark the accuracy ceiling of full fine-tuning rather than a
like-for-like comparison. Full fine-tuning still leads on three
datasets: SIM-Trans on SoyGlobal (70.69 against our 64.02)
and CSDNet on Cotton (57.92 against 55.42) and SoyGene
(70.82 against 70.53). Our model reaches its average while
deploying 123× fewer parameters across the ten datasets —
7.0 M of task-specific modules and heads against ten fully
fine-tuned CSDNet backbones — since their approach adapts
the whole backbone for each task. Part of SAW’s margin
reflects the pooled warmup corpus, which no competitor sees:
warmed up on the target dataset alone, SAW still gains 4.4
and 7.1 points over ILA++ on the two datasets probed in
Tab. XIII, with pooling contributing the remaining 1.7 and 4.9
points there. This highlights the strengths behind the proposed
semantic warmup with cross-image augmentations for efficient
adaptation.
Table VI makes the Swin comparison direct by deploying
our methods on the same Swin-B backbone at 448 × 448,
frozen, over three seeds. The ladder transfers: the warmup
adds 8.3 points over the adapter baseline and 14.9 over the
frozen head on average, reaching 74.87% — above our ViT
average of 73.5% — and it stabilizes the one dataset where
the adapter falls below the frozen head (SoyGlobal, 39.07%
with a 10.46 spread across seeds, restored to 64.61% with a
0.38 spread). Against full fine-tuning of the same backbone,
our result sits 0.8 points below CLE-ViT while topping it on
four of the ten datasets, and CSDNet and GAEor keep 4.2-

Fully fine-tuned Swin-B

Average

and 7.3-point average leads while adapting every backbone
parameter per task.
Additionally, these results demonstrate the scalability across
image resolutions of our proposed ILA variants. Our variants
improve the classification accuracy by at least 6.9 and up to
8.5 points on average across the ten datasets by increasing the
resolution from 224 to 448. This exceeds the mean improvement of 5.2 points across the rest of the methods, although
individual baselines come close (VPT-Deep gains 8.4 points).
These results combined with the reduced computational cost
of our method showcase the potential of our framework for
fine-grained tasks that require high-resolution.

C. Accuracy vs Computational Cost
We evaluate the trade-off between accuracy and computational cost across various PETL methods. In particular, we
compare the average top-1 accuracy across 10 UFGIR datasets
versus the computational cost in terms of task-specific trainable parameters, inference FLOPs and inference throughput.
We visualize these trade-offs in Fig. 8.
First, we highlight how the proposed ILA variants consistently increase the throughput and reduce the FLOPs compared
to other methods due to the reduction in processed tokens as a
consequence of the spatial downsampling. We also remark that
incorporating the Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) stage
into ILA++ improves accuracy by 5.9% without increasing
task trainable parameters or inference cost. The warmup does
add training compute: 100 extra epochs, run once and shared

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

12

Fig. 8. Accuracy vs inference floating-point operations (FLOPs in log-scale, on the left) and throughput (images per second, on the right) for various PETL
methods. The y-axis indicates average top-1 accuracy across 10 UFGIR datasets, the x-axis shows inference floating-point operations (FLOPs) in log scale,
and the marker size reflects the number of task-specific trainable parameters.

by every task. This margin is not a longer-training effect.
Training ILA++ for 150 epochs per task, the same budget
spent without the warmup, raises its ten-dataset average from
67.7% to 69.2% over one seed, still 4.3 points short of SAW’s
73.5%.
The previous best PETL method, FFVT, achieves an average
accuracy of 60.9% while SAW reaches 73.5%, with 8%
fewer FLOPs, 40% higher throughput, and 93% fewer trained
parameters per task. Additionally, while the previous state-ofthe-art, CSDNet, achieves a competitive accuracy of 68.4%,
it deploys 123× more parameters across the ten datasets
since all backbone parameters are fine-tuned per task. Overall,
these results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed
framework to improve recognition performance while reducing
processing costs in low-resource scenarios.

Dataset

The closest prior art to the warmup is ExPLoRA [62], which
also inserts an extended pretraining stage on the target domain
before parameter-efficient fine-tuning. It differs in three ways
that matter here: it unfreezes one or two transformer blocks
where we train adapters only, it uses a self-supervised reconstruction or distillation objective where we use a supervised
contrastive one, and it augments generically where DAMAS
swaps discriminative regions between images of the same
class. The ExPLoRA runs here extend pretraining per dataset
— LoRA of rank 8 on the attention projections, 300 epochs
of masked-autoencoder reconstruction from the released MAE
checkpoint — and then fine-tune the same LoRA for 100
epochs at a learning rate of 10−3 over three seeds. Table VII
compares them on all ten datasets, alongside three alternative
pretrained backbones adapted the same way.
Extended pretraining helps: ExPLoRA raises the MAE
backbone from 22.51% to 50.11% on average and overtakes
both CLIP and DINOv2. It remains below our result on
every dataset, by 15.8 points on average. The warmup leads
every row except Cotton, where the DINOv2 backbone is

E. Ablations
1) Design of Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS)
Branch: We perform ablation experiments to evaluate the
importance of the Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS)
branch in our ILA++ framework. We observe that completely
removing the RSDS branch results in a collapsed representation, with accuracy dropping to near-zero levels, despite
the presence of both adapters and ILA. This confirms that

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

13

the RSDS branch residual signal propagation is essential for
enabling the overall architecture to learn.
Second, we examine the impact of removing spatial downsampling from the RSDS path by padding both the main and
residual branches to match dimensions. This configuration fails
to improve upon the baseline with adapters, highlighting the
need for inductive biases in the form of hierarchical feature
extraction.
Finally, we assess various design choices for the RSDS
operator. All alternatives fail to improve upon the adapter
baseline. In particular, depth-wise convolution with Xavier
initialization performs poorly, showing the importance of
initialization for proper residual propagation. In contrast, our
default design incorporating depth-wise convolution initialized
to near one values consistently outperforms all other variants, surpassing the adapter baseline by 8.5 and 8.0 points,
respectively. This confirms that a learnable, identity-preserving
initialization is essential for smoothly bridging the spatial mismatch introduced by downsampling while preserving feature
integrity.
These results validate the design choices made in the RSDS
as a necessary component within ILA, enabling the inclusion
of hierarchical biases for feature extraction in UFGIR.

by at least 5.7 and up to 9.1 points, while reducing FLOPs

Although placing adapters deeper (e.g., after layers 5 and

as the default for ILA++, offering a strong balance between
efficiency and performance.

3) Impact of ILA Convolution Kernel Size: We study the
effects of the convolution kernel size within the ILA main and
residual branches, which directly controls the downsampling
rate in Tab. X. While employing a larger kernel size (e.g.,
kernel size 7) can improve accuracy in certain datasets and
reduce FLOPs due to the stronger downsampling ratio, the
kernel-7 configuration was not run on SoyGlobal, so its
behavior there is unmeasured. We opt for kernel size 3 due to
its stable training and balanced performance.
4) Impact of the Backbone Choice: To assess the effectiveness of the proposed methods across backbones, we deploy
them on DeiT [63] and DeiT3 [64] backbones in Tab. XI.
Results indicate that ILA++ and SAW consistently improve
upon the baseline regardless of the backbone choice. We
highlight how SAW can boost accuracy by up to 31.5 points
when combined with the DeiT3 backbone on the challenging
SoyGlobal dataset. These results demonstrate the effectiveness
of the proposed hierarchical feature extraction and semantic
warmup stage as robust techniques to distinguish ultra-fine
distinctions in parameter-efficient settings.

5) The Warmup Without ILA: Every warmup run reported
so far is built on ILA, so the warmup could be read as a
property of that module rather than a method in its own right.
Table XV settles this by applying it to a plain adapter with
no inter-layer module anywhere in the network. Average top1 accuracy rises from 53.42% to 62.20%, a gain of 8.78
points that holds on all ten datasets and ranges from 3.46 on
SoyAgeing to 15.34 on SoyLocal. The two arms are trained
identically at a fixed learning rate over three seeds, and differ
only in whether the warmup checkpoint is loaded.
The gain is larger than the 6.7 points the warmup adds on
top of ILA at the same 224 × 224 resolution, so the two are
complementary rather than one depending on the other. This is
what the framing in Sec. V-A predicts: the warmup addresses
a mismatch between the pretrained representation and cultivar
recognition, which is a property of the backbone and not of
the module placed on top of it.
6) Impact of Data Augmentation Strategy in SAW: We
compare data augmentation strategies to produce positives for
the contrastive loss function during the SAW stage in Tab. XII.
First, we remark how incorporating generic data augmentation
in the form of random cropping, color jittering, greyscale
or blur, as used in previous works [34], [35] can lead to
a significant performance degradation. This is because these
generic augmentations do not take into consideration the subtle
distinctions required for distinguishing fine-grained categories.
Second, incorporating data-aware cropping or masking [27],
[57], where an image region is zoomed in or substituted with
a value based on an attention mechanism, can be beneficial
for datasets with extreme data scarcity such as SoyLocal.
However, its benefits are limited compared to cross-image
data augmentation [38], [65] where attentive regions of two
images are swapped. This is because the additional diversity

SoyLocal
Average

+3.46
+6.46
+5.99
+8.79
+7.64
+8.12
+10.06
+12.36
+15.34
+8.78

introduced through swapping discriminative details between
two images from the same category allows the network to
directly learn from the large intra-class diversity observed in
UFGIR tasks.
7) Impact of Data Diversity in SAW Stage: We verify the
effect of data diversity during the warmup stage in Tab. XIII.
Our results show that incorporating the proposed DAMAS
significantly boosts the classification performance regardless
of if we warmup using a single-dataset before fine-tuning on
the same dataset or if we warmup using a combination of all
datasets before fine-tuning on each dataset individually. However, increasing data diversity by combining multiple datasets
is particularly effective at boosting performance on datasets
with very limited number of samples, such as SoyLocal,
where the proposed method employing a combined dataset can
further boost accuracy by 4.9%. This shows the importance of
data diversity during the warmup stage.
8) Impact of Loss Function used in SAW Stage: We
compare loss functions used during the adaptation stage in
Tab. XIV. SelfCon [37] incorporates representations from
intermediate layers of the network as additional positives for
the contrastive loss, resulting in a small increase in performance in SoyGlobal. However, processing these additional
positives increase the overall training cost so we opt to employ
SupCon [35], which employs only augmentations and sameclass images as positives, due its balance between accuracy
and cost.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

VI. C ONCLUSION
This work presents a novel framework for parameterefficient ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) that
combines a downsampling Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) module
with Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) stage. The ILA
module introduces inductive bias in the form of hierarchical
feature extraction for frozen Vision Transformers, significantly
reducing deployment parameters and inference cost. To address the semantic gap between generic pretraining and finegrained tasks we proposed SAW, an initialization strategy
that employs a novel Dual-Attention-driven Mix Augmentation
SupCon (DAMAS) objective to align adapter features with the
frozen backbone in a data-efficient manner.
The proposed framework incorporating ILA and SAW
achieves significant accuracy gains across ten UFGIR benchmarks while reducing inference cost and parameter overhead
compared to existing methods. These results highlight the
effectiveness of combining inductive biases with task-specific
pretraining for parameter-efficient adaptation in scenarios with
limited data.

=== PAPER: Ternary LLM Decoders for Multilingual Speech Recognition with a Frozen Encoder and a Linear Projector ===

TERNARY LLM DECODERS FOR MULTILINGUAL SPEECH RECOGNITION WITH A
FROZEN ENCODER AND A LINEAR PROJECTOR
Edwin Arkel Rios

ABSTRACT
Speech large language models attach a speech encoder to a text decoder through a small projector; the decoder holds most of the parameters. Can it be ternary? We train only a linear projector between
a frozen Whisper encoder and a frozen decoder, on 12 WorldSpeech
locales, for three ternary decoders and one FP16 decoder. One pair
differs only in precision: Ternary-Bonsai-4B against the Qwen3-4B
it was distilled from. On that pair the character error rate difference on the selection split is -1.31 points (Wilcoxon p = 0.30),
with a minimum detectable effect of 3.5 to 6.8 points. The ternary
arm ships at 1.71 bits per weight and 5.2× less decoder memory as
built. Per-utterance error profiles match across precisions: insertions
carry about half of all errors for both decoders, and the ternary decoder produces no more runaway, looping or off-language output.
The ternary decoders do not overfit less than the FP16 twin, they are
noisier late in training, and one Tamil run collapsed. A ternary decoder holds basic multilingual recognition at the 4B scale; the open
question is stability, not accuracy.
Index Terms— ternary quantization, speech LLM, multilingual
ASR, frozen decoder, low-resource languages
1. INTRODUCTION
A speech large language model (LLM) for recognition is usually
three parts: a speech encoder, a text LLM decoder, and a projector
that maps encoder frames into the decoder’s embedding space [1, 2].
The decoder holds most of the parameters and most of the memory
traffic at decode time, so it is the part worth compressing. Ternary
LLMs store every linear weight as one of three values [3, 4], and
open ternary decoders now exist at useful sizes: Falcon-E trained
with quantization-aware training [5], and Ternary-Bonsai distilled
from Qwen3 [6, 7]. No published result places one of them behind a
speech encoder.
This paper does that, in the simplest setting where the comparison is clean. Encoder and decoder stay frozen, and only a linear
projector is trained, once per locale. Because the decoder is frozen
in every arm, the trained capacity is the same projector everywhere,
and the only thing a ternary arm changes is the decoder it must drive.
We train 4 ternary arms and one FP16 arm on 12 WorldSpeech locales spanning four resource tiers, and evaluate on three populations.
The contributions are four. First, the first measurement of a
ternary LLM decoder in a speech LLM, with the decoder’s cost
stated in bits per weight under two accountings. Second, an identified precision comparison: Ternary-Bonsai-4B against the Qwen34B it was distilled from, on the same encoder, projector and data,
where no population shows a detectable difference and the minimum detectable effect is stated. Third, the error profile from 215
sets of per-utterance hypotheses: which error classes a ternary decoder makes, and that it hallucinates no more than its twin. Fourth,
the training-dynamics side: ternary decoders do not overfit less, they

2. SETUP
Architecture. Every arm has the shape of SLAM-ASR [1]: a frozen
Whisper encoder [8], a linear projector with fixed 4× temporal
downsampling, and a frozen decoder. The projector is the only
trained module. Audio embeddings replace placeholder tokens in
the decoder’s input sequence.
Arms. Table 1 lists the decoders. Ternary-Bonsai-1.7B and 4B
are distilled from Qwen3 and keep the Qwen tokenizer; Falcon-E1B is trained ternary from the start. The FP16 arm is Qwen3-4B
on Whisper-medium. One pair is identified: Ternary-Bonsai-4B and
Qwen3-4B on Whisper-medium share the hidden size, the tokenizer
and the training recipe, so precision is the only difference. The turbo
arms and the 1.7B arm have no FP16 twin. Falcon-E cannot have
one, because its FP16 counterpart is a quantization-aware master,
not a trained model.
Cost accounting. A ternary weight carries log2 3 = 1.58 bits.
Bonsai adds one 16-bit scale per group of 128 weights, Falcon-E
one per tensor. Norms and any untied head stay in FP16. “Shipped”
counts the checkpoint as published. “As built” adds the three audio
placeholder tokens the composite appends to the embedding table.
Those rows are dense, so the whole tied table becomes FP16. That
moves Bonsai-4B from 1.71 to 3.09 bits per weight and Bonsai-1.7B
to 4.29, because linear layers hold 90% of the 4B parameters and
a smaller share of the 1.7B. Both numbers are stated because the
second is what runs, and the first is what a concatenating architecture
would keep. As built, the Bonsai-4B decoder is 1,482 MB against
7,672 MB for Qwen3-4B.
Data and training. WorldSpeech [9] supplies the 12 training
locales, assigned to four resource tiers by Whisper training hours:
high, mid, low and very low. Each arm trains one projector per locale, at the same steps, batch and seed. The ternary arms train at
learning rates of 0.003 to 0.01, searched on Tamil; the FP16 arm
trains at 0.001, unsearched. That gap of 3 to 10× is the confound
that every overfitting comparison below carries. Character error rate

880
1,482
556
7,672

(CER) is the metric throughout.
Populations and tests. Three populations are compared: the selection split, where the best checkpoint is chosen; the FLEURS test
set [10], available for 11 of the locales; and the in-domain WorldSpeech test set. Each ternary arm is paired with the FP16 arm per
locale. We report the mean and median paired difference, a bootstrap 95% confidence interval on the mean, a two-sided Wilcoxon
signed-rank p [11], a Holm correction across the four arms [12], and
the minimum detectable effect (MDE) at 80% power. A positive
difference means the ternary arm is worse.
Error profile. From every evaluation’s per-utterance hypotheses we split CER into substitutions, deletions and insertions by
character-level alignment, and count three kinds of degenerate output: a runaway hypothesis longer than twice its reference, a loop
with one word repeated four times or a word pair three times in
a row, and an off-language hypothesis whose identified language
differs from its own reference’s. Recomputed corpus CER matches
the evaluation harness within 0.005 on all 215 runs.
3. RESULTS
3.1. Does a ternary decoder hold basic recognition?
Table 2 gives the best selection-split CER per locale for every arm,
and Table 3 the paired contrasts. On the identified pair, no population shows a detectable difference. The mean difference is -1.31
CER on the selection split (p = 0.30), -1.77 on FLEURS (p = 0.41)
and -2.59 in domain (p = 0.34), with the ternary arm ahead in 8 of
12 locales. The MDE is 3.5 to 6.8 CER, so the data exclude a large
penalty and nothing smaller. Medians over the 12 locales are 14.67
CER for the ternary arm and 13.96 for FP16; the turbo arms sit at
14.14 and 14.91, Falcon-E at 19.37.
The one arm that loses is Falcon-E-1B, at 2.71 bits per weight:
4.78 CER worse on average on the selection split, worse in 11 of
12 locales, Holm p = 0.02; 10.34 worse in domain (Holm p <
0.01) and 7.00 on FLEURS (Holm p = 0.01). Its decoder has
1.7B parameters against 4B and a different tokenizer, so this is a
model difference, not a precision effect. The turbo arms have no
twin; Turbo/Bonsai-4B is -3.81 CER ahead of the medium FP16 arm
(p = 0.01, Holm p = 0.11), and the best ternary arm per locale is
-4.02 ahead (p < 0.01, Holm p = 0.06). Both gains come with a
larger encoder, so they show that a ternary decoder does not cap the
system, not that it improves it.
3.2. Do ternary decoders overfit less?
A frozen ternary decoder has less representational freedom, so one
might expect the projector to overfit it less. Table 4 tests this on the
selection curves. The late rise is the final CER minus the best CER of
a run; late noise is the standard deviation of CER over the last 30% of
evaluations. No ternary arm overfits less than the FP16 twin. The
identified arm rises 3.45 CER at the median against 1.87 for FP16, a
paired difference of 0.92 (p = 0.09, worse in 8 of 12 locales; 0.89
and p = 0.17 without Tamil). The other arms sit at zero. Late noise
is higher in every ternary arm; on the identified pair it is 0.85 against
0.50 CER at the median, a paired difference of 0.27 (p = 0.03).
In evaluation loss, comparable across the Qwen-tokenizer arms, the
identified pair differs by 0.0002 (p = 0.68).
The learning-rate gap explains the direction. The ternary arms
train 3 to 10× faster than the FP16 arm, which alone predicts a larger
late rise and more noise. Removing that confound needs an FP16

3.3. Where does overfitting sit, and what collapsed?
Figure 1 shows every selection curve. Overfitting in loss terms follows the resource tier, not the decoder. The median eval-loss rise
after its minimum, per language then per tier over all five arms, is
0.101 in the very-low tier, 0.081 low, 0.012 mid and 0.001 high. Every arm, including FP16, overfits the two very-low-resource locales.
One run collapsed. Medium/Bonsai-4B on Tamil reached 32.2
CER at step 210 of 1000 and ended at 69.9, while its FP16 twin went
from 44.6 to 52.8. Training loss kept falling and gradient norms
stayed small, so this is overfitting that turned into degenerate decoding, not an optimisation failure. It is one cell at one seed, trained at
the learning rate that was searched on this very locale, and it is the
one degeneration-shaped event in 48 ternary runs.
3.4. Which errors does a ternary decoder make?
A ternary decoder could hold CER while changing what the errors
are, for instance by leaning on its language prior and inserting text
the audio does not contain. Table 5 gives the in-domain error profile.
The profile is the same for both precisions. Insertions carry 48% of
CER for the ternary arm and 47% for FP16 in the median locale (p =
0.47), and they are the largest error class for every arm. Runaway
hypotheses are 1.6% of utterances against 2.0% (p = 0.37) and carry
12% and 16% of CER; loops are 1.0% against 0.7% (p = 0.52); offlanguage output is 1.2% against 1.4% (p = 0.04). Urdu is the one

steps to within 1.5× the best CER is 0.23 (permutation p = 0.04),
and on audio hours to the same point 0.43 (p < 0.01). On 90 heldout country varieties of the training locales, the variety CER tracks

4. LIMITATIONS
The identified comparison covers one pair at one scale, Whispermedium with a 4B decoder, and the MDE of 3.5 to 6.8 CER bounds
what it can say. The learning rates were searched for the ternary
arms and not for FP16, so the overfitting and noise differences are
upper bounds on any precision effect. Every cell is one seed, so
the Tamil collapse is an observation and not a rate. The cost table
counts weight bits from the model configuration and the accounting
stated above; it does not report measured latency, which depends
on a ternary kernel that the evaluation host did not use. The error

1.2
1.4
1.5
1.8
1.4

profile uses fixed thresholds for runaway and loop output and a text
language identifier that is weak on short hypotheses and on Seselwa.
Two held-out country-variety sets, Pakistani English and Sri Lankan
Tamil, degenerate for every arm alike, FP16 included, the first into
runaway output and the second into deletions; they enter no contrast.
5. CONCLUSION
A ternary LLM decoder behind a frozen Whisper encoder, driven by
a single trained linear projector, holds basic multilingual recognition
at the 4B scale on 12 locales, at 1.71 bits per weight shipped and
5.2× less decoder memory as built. Against the FP16 decoder it was
distilled from, no population shows a detectable difference. Its errors
are the same errors: insertions carry about half of CER for both precisions, and the ternary decoder produces no more runaway, looping
or off-language output than its twin. What the ternary decoders do
not give is a training benefit: they overfit at least as much, they are
noisier late in training, and one run collapsed. The next measurements are an FP16 learning-rate search to remove the confound and
seeds on the collapsing cell.

=== PAPER: Cross-Layer Cache Aggregation for Token Reduction in Ultra-Fine-Grained Image Recognition ===

Cross-Layer Cache Aggregation for Token
Reduction in Ultra-Fine-Grained Image Recognition
Edwin Arkel Rios† , Jansen Christopher Yuanda‡ , Vincent Leon Ghanz‡ , Cheng-Wei Yu† , Bo-Cheng Lai† , Min-Chun Hu‡
†
National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Taiwan, ‡ National Tsing Hua University, Taiwan

Abstract—Ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) is a
challenging task that involves classifying images within a macrocategory. While traditional FGIR deals with classifying different
species, UFGIR goes beyond by classifying sub-categories within
a species such as cultivars of a plant. In recent times the usage
of Vision Transformer-based backbones has allowed methods to
obtain outstanding recognition performances in this task but this
comes at a significant cost in terms of computation specially
since this task significantly benefits from incorporating higher
resolution images. Therefore, techniques such as token reduction
have emerged to reduce the computational cost. However, dropping tokens leads to loss of essential information for fine-grained
categories, specially as the token keep rate is reduced. Therefore,
to counteract the loss of information brought by the usage of
token reduction we propose a novel Cross-Layer Aggregation
Classification Head and a Cross-Layer Cache mechanism to
recover and access information from previous layers in later
locations. Extensive experiments covering more than 2000 runs
across diverse settings including 5 datasets, 9 backbones, 7 token
reduction methods, 5 keep rates, and 2 image sizes demonstrate
the effectiveness of the proposed plug-and-play modules and
allow us to push the boundaries of accuracy vs cost for UFGIR
by reducing the kept tokens to extremely low ratios of up to
10% while maintaining a competitive accuracy to state-of-the-art
models. Code is available at: https://github.com/arkel23/CLCA
Index Terms—Efficient, Vision Transformer, Fine Grained
Visual Analysis, Model Compression, Image Classification.

I. I NTRODUCTION
Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) involves classifying
images into sub-categories within a larger macro-category [1].
While conventional FGIR [1] classifies objects usually up to
species-level granularity, ultra-FGIR (UFGIR) may categorize
classes at a finer level, such as cultivars of a plant. It has
practical applications in fields such as agriculture [2].
It is challenging due to small inter-class and large intraclass variations, but in recent years there has been a significant
improvement in performance in terms of accuracy. Previous
work has noted how this may be driven by backbone choices
[3]. In particular, the Vision Transformer (ViT) [4] has brought
a large performance improvement across tasks [5], [6]; the
global receptive field of the ViT’s attention mechanism provides it an advantage in handling long-range dependencies,
allowing it to effectively discover and learn dependencies
between discriminative features within an image [3].
This work was supported by the National Science and Technology Council,
Taiwan, under grant NSTC 111-2221-E-A49-092-MY3, NSTC 113-2640-EA49-005, NSTC 112-2221-E-007-079-MY3 and NSTC 113-2218-E-007-020.
We thank NYCU HPC for providing computational and storage resources.

However, this accuracy improvement has come at a cost
in terms of computational resources required for training and
inference [3]. ViT-based models have higher FLOPs compared
to equivalent CNN-based models, specially as the image size
is increased. This is due to the quadratic complexity O(N 2 )
of the self-attention mechanism with respect to the sequence
length N . Specialized FGIR models employing ViT as a
backbone [5] may even exhibit cubic complexity O(N 3 ) due
to their usage of aggregated attention [7].
Employing higher-resolution images in FGIR is common in
order to identify the small differences between classes [8], [9].
In order to effectively make use of current and next-generation
scaled models, specially in low-resource settings, it is crucial
to increase the efficiency of such ViT-based models; token
reduction is a promising direction to increase their efficiency.
Token Reduction (TR) proposes dropping [10], [11] or
combining [12], [13] unimportant tokens from the sequence
in order to reduce the number of tokens to be processed; it is
also known as token or input pruning. In computer vision these
tokens represent image patches. By reducing the number the
tokens the FLOPs, latency, and memory consumption could
be reduced, while throughput can be increased [10], [14].
Different TR methods use different criteria for selecting or
merging tokens; some use attention [11], [15], while others
employ learnable modules [10], [16]. However, regardless of
the TR selection criteria, the process of TR inevitably leads to
the loss of valuable information for discrimination, specially
as the keep ratio (KR) of tokens is reduced.
Therefore, to counteract the loss of information brought
by the TR process we propose two novel mechanisms: 1)
a Cross-Layer Aggregation (CLA) Classification Head which
incorporates skip connections for intermediate features of a
transformer directly into the classification module, and 2) a
Cross-Layer Cache (CLC) structure to store and recover crosslayer information vital for fine-grained classification.
We refer to our full method method, incorporating both the
CLA Head and the CLC, as Cross-Layer Cache Aggregation
(CLCA). CLCA allows us to push the boundary of TR
methods, by employing extremely low token keep ratios (KR)
of up to 10%, while keeping an accuracy that is on-par with (or
even surpass in certain cases) state-of-the-art (SotA) methods
in UFGIR. We demonstrate the performance improvement
brought by our proposed contributions with different token
KRs, and image sizes for the SoyGene dataset [2] in Fig. 1.
Our contributions are as follows:

© 20XX IEEE. Personal use of this material is permitted. Permission from IEEE must be obtained for all other uses, in any current or future media,
including reprinting/republishing this material for advertising or promotional purposes, creating new collective works, for resale or redistribution to servers
or lists, or reuse of any copyrighted component of this work in other works..

For instance, DynamicViT [10] drops tokens with a static keep
rate based on decisions predicted by a learnable MLP module,
while ATS [11] drops tokens with a dynamic keep rate based
on attention scores. Conversely, token merging reduces the
total number of tokens by combining multiple tokens into one.
Zeng et al. [19] implement this via the DPC-KNN clustering
algorithm, while Zong et al. [16] adopt a learnable weight
approach, resulting in slimmed rather than dropped tokens.
III. P ROPOSED M ETHOD : CLCA

Fig. 1. Accuracy vs FLOPs for DeiT3 [17] with EViT [15] token reduction
method with (CLCA) and without (Base) our proposed modules on the
SoyGene dataset [2] as a function of different keep rates (10, 25, 50, 70
and 100%) and image sizes (in parenthesis).

1) We propose a novel Cross-Layer Aggregation Classification (CLA) Head to expedite information from
intermediate layers of a transformer directly into the
classification module.
2) We propose the Cross-Layer Cache (CLC) structure to
counteract the loss of information brought by token
reduction. The CLC allows us to access features from
previous layer at deeper layers in order to facilitate finegrained classification.
3) We conduct extensive experiments on 5 UFGIR datasets
with a variety of TR schemes, token keep rates, and
pretrained backbones. Across more than 2000 runs, the
proposed model, CLCA, combining the CLA and CLC
plug-and-play modules, significantly and consistently
improves the accuracy across diverse settings, while incurring minimal increase in cost. Furthermore, it enables
us to reduce the TR KR up to 10%, drastically reducing
computational costs, while maintaining an accuracy that
is on par with models with much higher cost as measured
by the number of floating-point-operations (FLOPs).
II. R ELATED W ORK
A. Ultra-Fine-Grained Image Recognition
UFGIR methods make use of generic image recognition
backbones and equip them with modules to select and aggregate discriminative features [5], [6] or employ loss functions
and tasks [18] to guide models to more effectively make use
of fine-grained features.
However, many of these methods employ transformer-based
backbones with a large computational cost, specially as the
image size increases due to the quadratic complexity of the
self-attention operator.
B. Token Reduction
Token reduction methods can be broadly categorized into
token pruning and token merging. Token pruning involves the
dropping of a number of uninformative tokens at certain layers.

The overview of our proposed method is shown in Fig. 2.
Our method is based on a generic Vision Transformer with
token reduction. The transformer encoder blocks are divided
into four groups based on the token reduction locations and
the last layer. Features in each group are processed using
a series of encoder blocks followed by a token reduction
and cache recovery operations. We aggregate intermediate
features corresponding to the outputs of the CLS token of each
group. These features are first normalized, then cross-layer
channel-wise information is aggregated through a depth-wise
convolution. We incorporate a non-linear activation function
to model complex relations between features. Finally, a pointwise convolution projects the aggregated features into a vector
of classification predictions.
A. Vision Transformer Encoder Group with Token Reduction
Images are patchified using a convolution with kernel size
P and flattened into a 1D sequence of D channels with length
N = (S1 /P ) × (S2 /P ), where S1 and S2 represent the image
width and height. A learnable CLS token is appended at the
start of the sequence. Learnable positional embeddings are
added to incorporate spatial information.
This sequence is then processed through a series of L transformer encoder blocks, divided into g groups, each consisting
of multi-head self-attention (MHSA) and position-wise feedforward networks (PWFFN).
At the last encoder block of each group we reduce tokens
according to each TR scheme. Our method can be combined
with most token reduction methodologies but we focus on
EViT [15]. EViT selects tokens to keep based on the average
attention-score of the CLS token with respect to the other
tokens as computed in the MHSA block.
Specifically, the top-k tokens with highest attention based
on the CLS token attention A0 are kept and the remaining
tokens are merged using a weighted average operation. Then
the kept tokens and the fused token will be concatenated and
forwarded to the PWFFN block as normally.
B. Cross-Layer Aggregation (CLA) Head
To prevent information loss we fast-forward intermediate
features of the network directly into the classification layer.
Specifically, we forward the CLS token output of each encoder
group (after each reduction location and the last layer) into the
proposed CLA Head, shown in the right block of Fig. 2.
In the CLA Head we concatenate and reshape the tokens
into a sequence of size g, denoted as CLS ∈ RD×g , before

Fig. 2. From top to bottom, on the left is the overview for a ViT with the proposed Cross-Layer Cache Aggregation (CLCA). A learnable CLS and CrossLayer Register (CLR) tokens are attached to a sequence of local features corresponding to image patches. This sequence is passed through a series of ViT
Encoder Groups equipped with the proposed Cross-Layer Cache (CLC). Each Encoder Group is composed of a series of transformer encoder blocks followed
by a reduction block that drops tokens at the end. After each encoder block we cache the spatial Global Average Pooling (GAP) of the local features, the
tokens surrounded by the green dashed box, and the last token in the sequence (the CLR). After the reduction block we recover intermediate features from
previous layers from the CLC, empty it, and then cache the current layer outputs. We repeat the process for each encoder group. Thereafter, the CLS token
outputs of each Encoder Group are processed by the Cross-Layer Aggregation (CLA) Classification Head. These CLS tokens are first normalized using a
BatchNorm layer, then forwarded through a Depth-Wise Convolution layer that combines cross-layer features for each channel separately. A second BatchNorm
and activation function are employed for learning non-linear interactions between intermediate features. Finally, the vector of features is passed through a
Point-Wise Convolution that outputs the classification predictions.

normalizing the features using a BatchNorm layer. Then, a
Depth-Wise Convolution is employed to aggregate channelwise interactions across layers.
Agg = DWConv(BN([CLSG1 ; CLSG2 ; . . . ; CLSGg ]))

(1)

The output is an aggregated token Agg ∈ RD·DWG where
DWG = 2 is the number of groups dedicated to each channel
in the depth-wise convolution. This aggregated token is passed
through a BatchNorm and GELU activation function in order
to model complex non-linear relationships between features.
Finally, a Point-Wise Convolution projects the aggregated features into a vector of class probabilities used for classification.
preds = PWConv(GELU(BN(Agg)))

(2)

C. Cross-Layer Cache (CLC)
Regardless of the TR scheme, the information encoded in
the original tokens is lost. Since reduction is applied from
early layers that process low-level features, it is unavoidable
to lose information that deeper layers may require to make
a distinction between fine-grained categories. Therefore, inspired by FFVT, [6] which aggregated low-level and middlelevel features from the network in the last encoder layer of a
ViT for improved classification performance, we incorporate a
Cross-Layer Cache (CLC) structure to store local and discriminative features from intermediate layers for future usage. An
overview of the CLC is shown in the middle block of Fig. 2.
Specifically, after each transformer encoder block in an
encoder group, we store the Global-Average-Pooling (GAP)

pooled local features of the model. Furthermore, inspired
by previous work which suggested that transformers need
registers [20] we append a learnable Cross-Layer Register
(CLR) at the end of the sequence. After each encoder block we
also store this CLR that aggregates discriminative information
across layers for future use.
After the reduction location or before the last layer, we
access the CLC to recover the lost information tokens by
appending the tokens stored in it to the original sequence.
This allows our model to counteract the information loss that
comes with token reduction and make usage of relevant crosslayer information for discrimination of fine-grained categories.
Finally, the CLC is emptied so as to not repeat tokens in future
recovery operations.

Our experiments use 5 ultra-fine-grained leaves datasets
collected by Yu et al. [2] where each category represents a

confirmed cultivar name attached to the seed obtained from
the genetic resource bank. Top-1 accuracies are averaged
across three seeds [22]. When applicable, the best values are
highlighted in bold and the second best are underlined.
We employ AdamW optimizer, 50 epochs, weight decay
of 0.05, batch size of 32, two image sizes (224, 448), nine
backbones (ViT [4], DeiT [23], DeiT3 IN1k & IN21k [17],
MIIL [24], MoCov3 [25], DINO [26], MAE [27], CLIP
LAION [28]) five different keep rates (100, 70, 50, 25, and
10%). The token reduction operations are done on the 4th, 7th,
and 10th of a ViT B-16 model with 12 layers, therefore we
recover tokens from the CLC after these same locations and
also after layer 11.
The proposed CLCA is agnostic to the TR method. We
mainly apply it with EViT [15], but we also apply it to methods
from all four different TR paradigms: DynamicViT [10] for
static pruning, ATS for dynamic pruning [11], SiT [16] and
PatchMerger [12] for soft merging, and DPCKNN [19] and
ToMe [13] for hard merging.

B. Comparison with State-of-the-Art in UFGIR
We compare our method against state-of-the-art (SotA)
methods using ViTs in the Cotton, SoyAgeing, and SoyGlobal
datasets in Table I. When compared to these we observe that
our method obtains superior performance in terms of accuracy
in the Cotton and SoyAgeing datasets. For the SoyGlobal
dataset our method obtains the second best accuracy but the
computational cost in terms of FLOPs is much lower.
C. Discussion on Why CLCA Works

V. R ESULTS AND D ISCUSSION
A. Comparison of CLCA with Different TR Schemes

Fig. 4. Ablation showing max magnitude of gradient across all layers of
the model during training for DeiT3 B-16 [17] on SoyLocal dataset with our
proposed modules and the corresponding top-1 accuracy.

Fig. 3. Distribution of accuracies for multiple keep rates (25, 50, and 70%)
on 9 different pretrained backbones as a function of the TR selection scheme
(and the baseline without TR) on SoyLocal and SoyGene datasets with and
without proposed CLCA.

In Fig. 4 we plot the gradients during training for our model.
Our analysis reveals that, prior to incorporating the proposed
components, the gradients exhibit significant instability.
Therefore, inspired by previous which suggests that skip
connections can smooth out the optimization landscape [29]
we incorporate information transfer in between 1) intermediate
layers of the network to the (CLA) classification head, and 2)
intermediate layers of an encoder group and the subsequent
block through the usage of the Cross-Layer Cache (CLC).
These proposed modules function as skip connections which
have been shown to ease the training by avoiding spurious
local optimum [30] and encouraging feature reuse [31].
Furthermore, in UFGIR the usage of low-, middle-, and
high-level features has been shown to be beneficial to distinguish between classes [6]. Our proposed modules provide
shorter connections to these features, which has been shown
to provide implicit deep supervision [32].

We plot the accuracy vs FLOPs as a function of image
size (IS) and keep rate (KR) for the SoyGene dataset with
EViT in Fig. 1. By incorporating CLCA the lower bound of
the accuracy is raised to 61% and to a maximum of 81%
compared to the original design which ranged from 30% to
67%. Models with CLCA are more robust to the TR KR and
the IS compared to the original while incurring a negligible
increase in cost as measured by the FLOPs.
These results are further validated by plotting the accuracies
distribution (across 9 backbones, 3 keep rates, 3 random seeds,
on 2 datasets) for different TR schemes with and without
CLCA, as shown in Fig. 3. Our method consistently improves
the performance across a wide variety of settings.

VI. C ONCLUSION
In this paper we propose Cross-Layer Cache Aggregation
(CLCA), a method to improve the performance of token
reduction in ultra-fine-grained image recognition tasks. The
proposed method integrates intermediate features from the network through a Cross-Layer Aggregation (CLA) classification
head for robust predictions, and Cross-Layer Cache (CLC) to
recover and integrate lost information from previous layers
that is lost under the token reduction paradigm. The proposed
methodology can be combined with a variety of mechanisms
to reduce tokens, massively reducing the computational cost
while consistently boosting the performance across different
UFGIR datasets under a variety of settings.

and S. Belongie, “Fine-Grained Image Analysis with Deep Learning: A

=== PAPER: Configurable Token Reduction in a Stream-Based Vision Transformer Accelerator on FPGA ===

Configurable Token Reduction in a Stream-Based Vision Transformer
Accelerator on FPGA
Anonymous authors
Paper under double-blind review

Abstract—Token reduction (TR) removes patch tokens from
the later layers of a Vision Transformer (ViT) and cuts its FLOPs
by a third to two thirds, but on a GPU the saving rarely reaches
the clock: at keep rate 0.25 and batch size 1, every one of seven
TR methods we measure on ViT-B runs 5% to 38% slower than
the unreduced model, and at batch size 64 latency falls by only
4% to 7%. An FPGA dataflow can shrink its work with the
token count, but which TR method to build is not obvious: over
eight methods, nine fine-grained datasets and three keep rates on
DeiT-Tiny, the best method changes with the dataset and the keep
rate, and the gap between the best and worst method reaches
46.3 points. This paper presents a design methodology that takes
a target dataset and an accuracy floor to a deployed accelerator
in four stages: dataset characterization, a software-level design
space exploration over TR methods and keep rates, operatingpoint selection, and hls4ml generation; and a stream-based, fully
on-chip ViT accelerator with sorting and pruning blocks that
support TopK and EViT behind one interface. The sweep shows
that these two methods stay within 0.09 points of the best method
on average at keep rate 0.7. On an Alveo U55C at 200 MHz with
288 × 288 inputs, TopK at keep rate 0.5, the operating point
a one-point accuracy floor selects on CUB, reduces latency by
37.8% and energy by 32.8% against the unreduced accelerator;
at keep rate 0.25 the reductions are 49.1%, 53.8% and 51.4% of
URAM. At 224 × 224 the design reaches 5291 frames per second
and 201 FPS/W, 19.5× the throughput and 7.0× the efficiency of
HeatViT on a ZCU102 (8.3× the throughput per available DSP).
Index Terms—Vision Transformer, token reduction, FPGA,
high-level synthesis, dataflow architecture

I. I NTRODUCTION
Vision Transformers (ViTs) [1] lead image recognition
benchmarks, and fine-grained tasks push them to larger inputs,
where self-attention’s cost grows with the square of the
token count. Token reduction (TR) methods keep only the
informative patch tokens after a few layers [2]–[4], and their
FLOPs savings are large. On a GPU those savings mostly do
not become time. On an RTX 3090 with ViT-B at 224 × 224,
the seven TR methods in Table III remove 57% to 66% of
the FLOPs at keep rate 0.25; at batch size 1 each of them
is slower than the unreduced model, by 5% to 38%, and at
batch size 64 the latency gain is 4% to 7%. The GPU’s fixed
parallelism, the gather and scatter that selection introduces,
and the small matrices that remain leave most of its resources
idle rather than free.
An FPGA can instead size its datapath to the tokens that
remain. Prior FPGA accelerators with token reduction [5]–
[7] show the latency gain, but each hard-wires one selection
rule. Our accuracy sweep says that is a real cost: across eight

TR methods, nine fine-grained datasets and three keep rates
on DeiT-Tiny, the best method differs across datasets at every
keep rate, and choosing the worst one costs up to 46.3 accuracy
points. The accelerator therefore needs a TR block whose
method is a configuration choice, and the choice needs data.
This paper makes three contributions.
• A design methodology from dataset to bitstream that
follows the toolflow pattern of FINN-R and ATHEENA—
measured fronts guide the designer, the tool generates the
design—and adds to it a characterization sweep over TR
methods and keep rates and an operating-point selection
against an accuracy floor. It is backed by a sweep of eight
TR methods on nine fine-grained datasets at keep rates
0.25, 0.5 and 0.7 on DeiT-Tiny (648 cells, three seeds
each): no single method wins at keep rates 0.5 and 0.7
(4 and 4 distinct winners over nine datasets), while the
sorting-based pair TopK and EViT stays within 0.09 and
0.64 points of the best method on average.
• A stream-based, fully on-chip DeiT-Tiny accelerator with
configurable TR: sorting and pruning blocks inserted after
blocks 4, 7 and 10 that support TopK and EViT (TopK
is the one measured here) with a partial selection sort
of O(KN ) cost; validity flags let the downstream blocks
process token streams of variable length. The design is
generated from the PyTorch model through an extended
hls4ml.
• Measurements on an Alveo U55C: at 288 × 288 inputs,
keep rate 0.5 reduces latency by 37.8% and energy by
32.8%, and keep rate 0.25 by 49.1%, 53.8% and 51.4%
of URAM; at 224×224 the accelerator delivers 5291 FPS
at 201 FPS/W, 19.5× and 7.0× HeatViT [5].
II. BACKGROUND AND R ELATED W ORK
A. Token Reduction
A ViT with N patch tokens and width D spends O(N 2 D)
per attention layer. TR methods drop or merge tokens at chosen
depths so later layers see fewer of them. Pruning methods
score tokens and keep the top K = ⌈r(N − 1)⌉ for keep
rate r: TopK uses the class token’s mean attention; EViT [2]
additionally averages the pruned tokens into one fusion token;
DynamicViT [4] learns a prediction head; ATS [8] samples
by the attention distribution. Merging methods combine similar tokens: ToMe [3] by bipartite matching on keys, DPCKNN [9], [10] by density-peak clustering, SiT [10] by a
learned soft assignment, and PatchMerger [11] by query-

B. FPGA Accelerators for ViTs with Token Reduction
HeatViT [5] adds a hardware token selector with a circular
buffer and a sorting network to a ViT accelerator on a
ZCU102. ViT-ToGo [6] prunes groups of spatially adjacent
tokens to cut sorting cost and regularize memory access. The
hybrid token selector of Yadav et al. [7] combines several
selection scores by weighted voting. Each fixes its selection
rule at design time, and each is a memory-based design that
moves activations between on-chip and off-chip memory. Each
of them also reports a single operating point rather than a way
to choose one. Yang et al. [12] describe a stream-based ViT
accelerator generated by hls4ml that keeps all weights and
activations on chip and connects layers by FIFOs; it has no
token reduction. Our accelerator starts from that architecture.
Toolflows such as fpgaConvNet [13], FINN-R [14] and
hls4ml [15] map a trained network to an FPGA under usergiven constraints and expose the design space—precision, parallelism, folding—for the designer to explore; FINN-R states
that it is to be used interactively to meet given constraints,
and ATHEENA [16] feeds a dataset statistic, the share of hard
samples, into its optimizer. The methodology below follows
that pattern: the choices that depend on the dataset are made
from measured fronts, and the tool generates the design.

similarities and a gather.

III. D ESIGN M ETHODOLOGY
Given a target dataset, a trained ViT and an accuracy floor,
the methodology answers which TR method and keep rate to
deploy, and produces the accelerator. The designer provides the
three inputs; the flow then characterizes the dataset, sweeps the
supported TR methods and keep rates in software, presents the
accuracy–keep-rate front from which the designer selects an
operating point, and generates the design with hls4ml. Design
space exploration here means what the toolflow literature
means by it, traversing the design space and selecting one
point against an application objective [13]; the selection is a
documented choice, not a rule.

B. Software-Level Design Space Exploration
A. Dataset Characterization
Fine-grained datasets differ in how far apart their classes
sit in feature space and how much each class varies. We
characterize a dataset by the mean cosine distance between
features of different classes, dinter , and within a class, dintra ,
computed from the pre-trained backbone’s class-token features
on the training set. Fig. 1 places 20 datasets on that plane for
DeiT-Tiny; the nine swept below span dinter from 0.07 to 0.27
and dintra from 0.04 to 0.15, from the soybean cultivars at
the low end to Dogs and Pets at the high end, and the coarse
datasets sit beyond them. The statistic places a new dataset
among the swept ones before any sweep; it does not pick the
method, and it does not predict it: the 4 different winners at
keep rate 0.7 in Fig. 1 are not separated by the two distances,
so the sweep of the next stage decides.

The design question is whether one TR method suffices,
and the answer sets what the accelerator must support. We
fine-tuned DeiT-Tiny with each of the eight methods on nine
fine-grained datasets—Aircraft [26], Cars [27], CUB [28],
Dogs [29], Moe [19], Pets [30], and SoyAgeing, SoyGene
and SoyLocal [17]—at keep rates 0.25, 0.5 and 0.7, three seeds
each, at 224×224. Every method reduces after blocks 4, 7 and
10 to the same K per block, the merging methods by merging
or clustering down to K tokens; the one exception is ToMe
at keep rate 0.25, which merges at six blocks (3, 4, 6, 7, 9,
10) with the per-block count that reaches the same final token
count; accuracy is the top-1 test accuracy of the FP16 PyTorch
model, averaged over seeds. Table I lists the best method and
the best-minus-worst gap per dataset and keep rate.
Three facts follow. First, the gaps are large: the worst

31.7 points on CUB at keep rate 0.7, so the choice matters.
Second, at keep rates 0.5 and 0.7 the winner changes with
the dataset (4 and 4 distinct winners over nine datasets),
while at keep rate 0.25 the merging method ToMe wins 6 of
nine. Third, the sorting-based pair TopK and EViT is a safe
hardware target at moderate reduction: the better of the two
trails the best of all eight by 0.09 points on average (at most
0.5) at keep rate 0.7, by 0.64 points (at most 1.6) at keep
rate 0.5, and by 2.1 points (at most 6.1) at keep rate 0.25.
Table II gives the regret per dataset. Repeating the sweep
with DeiT-Small at 224 and with DeiT-Tiny at 448, on the
same datasets, methods, keep rates and seeds, gives the same
ordering: DeiT-Small at 224 gives mean regrets of 0.08, 0.39
and 1.66 points at keep rates 0.7, 0.5 and 0.25, and DeiT-Tiny
at 448 gives 0.26, 0.63 and 3.03. Sorting-based methods share
one datapath, a score, a selection of the K highest scores and a
gather, and EViT adds only an accumulator; so the accelerator
of Section IV implements both behind one interface, and the
sweep over them is repeated for each new dataset.
C. Operating-Point Selection
The sweep yields, per method, accuracy as a function of
keep rate at the image size the hardware will run. The designer
states an accuracy floor relative to the unreduced model, as
prior work does with a loss of 0.5 or 1 point [5], and selects
the lowest keep rate whose accuracy stays above it; the front
serves as a guide, and an application that weighs latency higher

moves one step down it. Table IV is the front for CUB at
288 × 288, the hardware’s input size: TopK loses 0.30 points
at keep rate 0.5 for a 51% FLOPs reduction, gains 0.15 points
at keep rate 0.7, and loses 4.90 points at keep rate 0.25. Under
a 0.5-point floor the operating point is keep rate 0.5; a 1-point
floor gives the same; keep rate 0.25 needs a floor above 4.90
points.
D. Hardware Generation
The PyTorch model with the selected TR method, keep
rate and reduction blocks, together with the HLS configuration (precision, parallelism, memory partitioning), enters the
extended hls4ml. The converter recognizes the TR operators,
emits the HLS C++ for the Sorting and Pruning tasks with their
pragmas and a testbench against the PyTorch model, and Vitis
HLS and Vivado produce the bitstream. Image size, keep rate
and reduction positions are parameters of the flow; changing
the operating point changes a configuration, not the design.
This paper fixes the positions at blocks 4, 7 and 10 and does
not vary them. All designs use 8-bit fixed-point weights with
wider fixed-point intermediates.
IV. ACCELERATOR
A. Baseline Stream Architecture
Fig. 2 shows the encoder block of the baseline [12]. Data
enter over AXI-Stream, every weight sits in on-chip ROM,
and each block is a chain of tasks connected by FIFOs under
a block-level start/stop protocol, so all tasks run concurrently
in a pipeline. Multi-head self-attention (MHSA) is four tasks:
a linear layer for Q, K, V ; the scaled dot product QK ⊤
with an online softmax; the P V product with rescaling (PVOP in Fig. 2); and the output linear layer. Ping-pong (PIPO)
buffers hold Q, K, V for random access, shallow FIFOs pass
the short-lived softmax intermediates, and a stream-of-blocks
buffer holds the attention output, which the output linear layer
reads sequentially across tokens and randomly within a token.
A clone stream feeds the residual FIFO, whose depth must
cover two inputs so that the add does not deadlock against
the next input’s LayerNorm. The FFN’s two linear layers are
joined by a stream-of-blocks buffer of depth two.
B. Token Reduction Blocks
Fig. 3 adds two tasks to a block. The Sorting task reads
the class-token row of the attention from the QK task and
computes each patch token’s importance
∑︁ as the class-token
1
attention averaged over heads, sj = H
h Ah [0, j]. It keeps

Fig. 2. One encoder block of the baseline stream-based accelerator. FIFOs join the tasks; PIPO buffers hold Q, K and V ; SoB buffers hold the attention
output and the FFN hidden activations; FIFO-1 and FIFO-8 carry the residuals.

Fig. 3. The encoder block with token reduction. The Sorting block reads the class-token attention row from the QK task and writes the indices of the K
kept tokens to a FIFO; the Pruning block after the residual add forwards only those tokens, and for EViT appends the fusion token.

a running list of the K best scores and indices with a partial
selection sort: the list is initialized with the first K tokens,
each later token replaces the current minimum when its score
exceeds it, and after the last token the K indices are written
to a FIFO. The cost is O(KN ) comparisons in a sequential
pass, which fits the streaming order of the scores and avoids
a full sorting network; the selected set is identical to a full
sort’s. The Pruning task sits after the residual add. It forwards
the class token and the K selected patch tokens and drops
the rest, so the next block receives K + 1 tokens. For EViT
it also accumulates the dropped tokens into one fusion token,
divides once by the count and emits K + 2 tokens. At the next
reduction, fusion tokens compete as ordinary patch tokens.
Weights differ between TopK and EViT only in the fine-tuned
model; the hardware differs in the accumulator, so both come
from the same generator with one switch.
Because the token count changes at each reduction, every
downstream buffer keeps its static allocation and each token
carries a validity flag. Tasks iterate over the allocated length
and skip invalid entries, so the schedule of a block does not
depend on which tokens survived, only on how many. For the
12-block DeiT-Tiny we place reductions after blocks 4, 7 and
10, the placement used for the accuracy sweep, so the first
four blocks see every token and the savings compound over
the last eight.
V. E VALUATION
A. Setup
Designs are built with Vitis HLS and Vivado 2023.2 for
the Alveo U55C (XCVU47P: 746,880 LUTs, 1,493,760 FFs,
2,688 BRAM blocks, 320 URAM blocks, 5,952 DSPs, 16 GB
HBM2) at 200 MHz. The model is DeiT-Tiny (12 blocks, 3
heads, width 192, 5.7M parameters). The main experiment

uses 288 × 288 inputs, 325 tokens, where the quadratic attention term is large enough for token reduction to matter, with
TopK at keep rates 0.25, 0.5 and 0.7 after blocks 4, 7 and 10;
the baseline is the same accelerator without reduction. Latency
is the end-to-end time for one image, power is the Vivado postroute estimate, and energy is their product. Accuracy figures
are the FP16 software evaluation of the fine-tuned models, as
in Section III; the operating point follows Section III-C.
B. Latency, Energy and Resources
Table V reports the 288 × 288 designs. At keep rate 0.25,
latency falls from 430.6 ms to 219.3 ms (49.1%, a 1.96×
speedup) and energy from 9.10 J to 4.20 J (53.8%); at 0.5
the reductions are 37.8% and 32.8%, and at 0.7 still 20.7%
and 4.3%. Power is 21.1 W for the baseline and 19.2, 22.8
and 25.5 W at keep rates 0.25, 0.5 and 0.7: it rises with the
keep rate, as LUT usage does, and at keep rate 0.7 it exceeds
the baseline, which is why the energy saving there is 4.3%
against a 20.7% latency saving. The largest resource effect is
on URAM, which holds the activations: the baseline uses 45%
of it and keep rate 0.25 uses 21.88%, a 51.4% reduction, which
is the headroom for a larger model or a larger batch on the
same device. DSP usage rises from 17.50% to 19.99% for the
sorting and pruning logic, BRAM is flat, and LUT usage does
not follow the keep rate; each column is a separate synthesis
run and we do not attribute the differences. The three reduced
designs meet timing at 200 MHz except keep rate 0.7, which
misses by 57 ps.
C. Comparison with GPUs and HeatViT
Table VI compares throughput and efficiency at 224 × 224.
The accelerator reaches 5291 FPS and 201 FPS/W: 180× the
throughput and 42× the efficiency of a Jetson Orin Nano, 67×
and 103× a Tesla V100, and 19.5× and 7.0× HeatViT [5]

on a ZCU102 at 150 MHz. Two factors separate the FPGA
designs. The U55C is a data-center part with 5,952 DSPs and
460 GB/s of HBM2 against the ZCU102’s 2,520 DSPs and
DDR4, so it admits more parallelism; per available DSP the
throughput ratio is 8.3×. The prior works’ throughput and
power are as their authors report them; ours is the Vivado
post-route estimate. And the stream architecture keeps every
activation on chip, whereas HeatViT’s memory-based flow
moves them through off-chip memory; the GPU comparison
shows the same effect from the other side, where the reduced
token count leaves the fixed datapath underused.
D. Limitations
The hardware results cover one model, DeiT-Tiny, and one
device; the fully on-chip design bounds the model to what
the U55C’s URAM and BRAM hold, and larger ViTs need a
hybrid memory scheme. The table reports TopK; EViT shares
the sorting block and adds the fusion accumulator, and its
accelerator comes from the same generator, but we do not
report its measurements here. Merging methods, which win at
keep rate 0.25 in the sweep, need a different datapath and are
future work.
VI. C ONCLUSION
Token reduction pays on an FPGA dataflow when it does
not on a GPU, and the method and keep rate to deploy should
follow the data: characterize the dataset, sweep the supported
methods in software, select the operating point against an
accuracy floor, and let the tool generate the design. On nine

fine-grained datasets no single method wins at moderate keep
rates, and the sorting-based pair TopK and EViT is within 0.09
points of the best on average at keep rate 0.7. An hls4mlgenerated stream accelerator with configurable sorting and
pruning blocks turns the selected keep rate 0.5 into 37.8%
less latency and 32.8% less energy on an Alveo U55C, and
keep rate 0.25 into 49.1%, 53.8% and 51.4% less URAM;
it reaches 19.5× the throughput of the prior FPGA tokenreduction accelerator, 8.3× per available DSP.
